Integration theory of Lie-graph algebras Thanks: 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 22E65; Secondary 18M70, 18M85, 14D15, 16T10. The authors are supported by ANR-20-CE40-0016 HighAGT
Ricardo Campos, Bruno Vallette

Abstract
. We develop the Lie theory of Lie-admissible algebras whose product is enriched with higher operations modeled on directed graphs with a view to apply it to the deformation theories controlled by this kind of Lie algebras. We produce effective formulas for their exponential map, their gauge group structure and the action on Maurer–Cartan elements. The main motivation and range of applications lies in the deformation theory of types of bialgebras which is done in a sequel article. This work extends the case of pre-Lie algebra structures which appear in the deformation theory of operadic algebras.



Introduction

Integration theory of properadic deformation Lie algebras

Lie’s third theorem tells us how to integrate the infinitesimal symmetries structured as a Lie algebra into global symmetries composing a group. This integration process plays a key role in deformation theories that are controlled by differential graded Lie algebras where this group, called the
gauge group
, encodes the possible equivalences between the Maurer–Cartan elements. These data form the so-called
Deligne groupoid
.

The integration of differential graded Lie algebras arising from deformation complexes of properadic structures has remained an open problem for several years, despite the growing interest in
algebraic structures having operations with several inputs and outputs
[
MV09a
,
MV09b
,
CFL20
,
KS18
,
HLV21
,
LV22
,
KTV25
]
.
This stands in stark contrast to the operadic setting, where an additional
pre-Lie structure
on the deformation complex allows one to produce
effective
formulas for the Deligne groupoid of the governing differential graded Lie algebra
[
DSV16
,
DSV24
]
.

In this paper we address this gap in two steps. First, we introduce a new type of algebraic structure, called

Lie-graph algebras
, which is parametrised by directed graphs and which induces a Lie bracket. Then, we settle the complete integration theory for this new class of algebras of Lie-type.
Unlike the associative and pre-Lie cases, where the entire structure can be recovered by iterating a single generating operation, the operad governing Lie-graph algebras is not finitely generated (
Proposition

1.13
): one must take
all
operations into account simultaneously.
This lack of finite presentability makes both the theoretical analysis and the explicit computation of their Deligne groupoids substantially more challenging.

Our approach exploits the combinatorics of the underlying graph structure to produce explicit formulas for the gauge group of a Lie-graph algebra and for its gauge action on the Maurer–Cartan elements.
This framework applies in particular to the deformation complexes of gebras over properads, for which we obtain a complete and effective description of the associated Deligne groupoid, see
[
CV25
, Section 1]
.

Context and historical overview

A classical heuristic in deformation theory, dating back to Deligne, Drinfeld, Gerstenhaber, Quillen, and others
[
Dri14
]
, states that deformation problems over a field characteristic zero are governed by differential graded Lie algebras. This heuristic has been formalised via the equivalence of
\infty
-categories between differential graded Lie algebras and formal moduli problems by Lurie and Pridham
[
Lur11
,
Pri10
]
, see also the recent operadic proof providing point-set models by Le Grignou–Roca i Lucio
[
GiL23
]
.

Under this correspondence, given a differential graded Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},d,[\,,])
, the set of objects to be deformed is given by the set of Maurer–Cartan elements of
\mathfrak{g}

{\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})\coloneq\left\{\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\,|\,d\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=0\right\}\penalty\ ,}

while equivalent deformations are detected by the action of the gauge group
\Gamma
on
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
, which arises as
the integration of the degree 0 Lie sub-algebra into a Lie group by means of exponentiation. The data of the gauge group
\mathfrak{G}
(morphisms) together with its action on the set of Maurer–Cartan elements
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
(objects)
forms the
Deligne groupoid
of
\mathfrak{g}
.

While one can describe the Deligne groupoid in theoretical terms, in practice, for an arbitrary differential graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, one cannot provide very effective formulas for either the gauge group or its action since both rely on the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula
[
BF12
]
.
One case where the Deligne groupoid can be made fully explicit is in the situation where the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
actually arises as the skew-symmetrisation of an associative product
\star
, that is
[x,y]=x\star y-y\star x
. In this situation, the gauge group is actually obtained by the classical exponentiation of the elements of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\exp(x)=1+x+\tfrac{1}{2!}x\star x+\tfrac{1}{3!}x\star x\star x+\dots\in\mathfrak{G}=\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{0})\penalty\ .

In fact, it happens frequently in the context of deformations of algebraic structures that the Lie bracket is obtained by skew-symmetrisation of a binary product
\star
, but which is not necessarily associative.
In this case, the Maurer–Cartan equation takes up the particularly nice form

d\alpha+\alpha\star\alpha=0\penalty\ .

For instance, the deformation complex of homotopy
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structures, for a Koszul operad
\mathcal{P}
, on a fixed chain complex, carries the structure of a pre-Lie algebra, see
[
DSV16
]
.
The exponential analogy turns out to be fruitful: in
loc. cit.
, the second author, together with Dotsenko and Shadrin, used the concept of
pre-Lie exponentials

[
AG80
]
, which allowed them to address in an effective way the integration theory of pre-Lie algebras which has since then been applied successfully to
rational homotopy theory
[
CPRNW24
]
,
algebraic topology and algebraic geometry
[
Emp24
]
,
numerical analysis
[
BLBM23
]
, and renormalisation theory
[
BHZ19
,
BD24
]
.

Lie-graph algebras and their exponential groups

In the properadic context, one can identify a binary product
whose skew-symmetrisation yields the Lie bracket of interest. However, this product is just Lie-admissible and not pre-Lie in general.
It is therefore natural to ask how much further can the exponentiation techniques be pushed.
One first key observation is that, contrary to the pre-Lie case, all the possible iterations of this Lie-admissible product are not enough to produce a meaningful exponential map.
So the first main insight of this paper is to identify additional structural operations and to encode them in a new type of algebraic structure:
we introduce the notion of a
Lie-graph algebra
,
which is made up of operations parametrised by directed simple graphs.
This new type of algebras is governed by an operad that we denote by
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
, of which the operad
\mathrm{Lie}
(and even
\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}
) is a quotient. The crucial result from Sections
1.3
and
1.4
is that
the deformation complexes of properadic structures carry a canonical Lie-graph algebra structure
.

This range of applications motivates the development of an effective integration theory of Lie-graph algebras.
This forms the first main result of this paper: an explicit description of the gauge group of Lie algebras arising
from Lie-graph algebras via
Lie-graph exponential and logarithm maps
.

Theorem
2.22
.

For any
complete Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, there are isomorphism of complete topological groups between the gauge group and the so-called deformation gauge group:

\exp\ :\Gamma=\big(\mathfrak{g}_{0},\mathrm{BCH},0\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\leftrightarrows}}\left(\mathbb{1}+\mathfrak{g}_{0},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\right)=\mathfrak{G}\ :\ \mathrm{log}\ ,

where both the exponential map
\exp
and the associative product
\circledcirc
are explicitly given as sums of directed (respectively
2
-leveled) graphs.

While such a result might look similar in form to analogous classical one for associative and even pre-Lie algebras, the
conceptual approach leading to these
maps is very different. In the classical associative and pre-Lie cases, the exponential maps were obtained by summing over repeated iterations of the generating binary operation
\star
divided by some factor. On the other hand, in the Lie-graph setting, we have to sum over
all possible graphical operations
, divided by an appropriate combinatorial factor, see Definition
2.15
. There is a way to interpret this discrepancy: while the associative and pre-Lie operads are generated by their respective binary operations, that is not the case for the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
, which is not even finitely generated (Proposition
1.13
). Retrospectively, one can get the associative and pre-Lie exponential maps by the same sum over all the elements of the respective operads.

Pursuing this direction, we are able to provide the literature with an effective formula for the action of the (deformation) gauge group on Maurer–Cartan elements. This represents the second main result of the present paper.

Theorem
2.29
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the action of the (deformation) gauge group on the set of Maurer–Cartan elements
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
is given by the explicit formula

\exp(\lambda)\cdot\alpha\coloneq\lambda.\alpha=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)\ ,

where

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.83282pt}{3.53769pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.59468pt}{3.53769pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ +\ \cdots

is the sum of
bow-tie
graphs, that is
3
-leveled directed graphs with only one middle vertex labeled by the Maurer–Cartan element
\alpha
.

Once restricted to ladders and to rooted trees, this formula recovers respectively the associative and the pre-Lie cases but in a more conceptual form. The methods used in the various proofs here are worth mentioning since they are based on the classical real and finite dimensional Lie theory, unlike the previous works on this domain. In order to settle these new universal formulas, we consider the free Lie-graph algebra over the field of rational numbers, naturally provided by the operadic calculus. We embed it into the one with real coefficients that we truncate under its canonical filtration in order to obtain a finite dimensional real Lie algebra.
Finally, in order to prove the last aforementioned theorem, we give a new conceptual treatment of the
differential trick
, which is a method to internalise the differential map into a given algebraic structure.

Applications

In a sequel article
[
CV25
]
, we apply this new universal integration theory to the
differential graded Lie algebras coming from properadic deformation theory.
We describe there the Deligne groupoid. We give a gauge theoretical explanation of the homotopy transfer theorem, emphasizing its perturbative nature. And, we generalise the Koszul hierarchy and the twisting procedure from the operadic case to the properadic case.
Theses results apply to a wide range of recently studied properadic structures appearing in algebra, geometry, topology, and mathematical physics.

Conventions

For a uniform and light presentation, we work over a ground field
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic
0
though many results hold under weaker hypotheses.
The underlying category is that of differential
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{k}
-vector spaces, using the homological degree convention, for which differentials have degree
-1
.
The symmetric groups are denoted by
\mathbb{S}_{n}
.
We use the conventions of
[
MV09a
,
MV09b
,
HLV21
]
for properads: for instance, we draw graphs with inputs at the top and outputs at the bottom. All the graphs present here will be directed by a global flow going from top to bottom.

1.
Algebraic structures on convolution algebras

In the first subsection, we start by recalling the main elements of the properadic calculus.
After that, we introduce a new algebraic structure, dubbed
Lie-graph algebra
for its connection with Lie algebras, which encodes all the natural operations acting on the totalisation of the underlying space of a properad.
In the last subsection, we introduce compatible complete topologies which will allow us to develop the integration theory of
Lie-graph algebras in the next section.

1.1.
Properadic calculus

Recall that an
\mathbb{S}
-bimodule is a collection
\{\mathcal{M}(m,n)\}_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
of right
\mathbb{S}_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}
-modules; they are meant to encode operations with
n
inputs and
m
outputs.
We consider the monoidal category

\big(\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}\mathsf{bimod},\allowbreak\boxtimes,\allowbreak\mathcal{I}\big)
of
\mathbb{S}
-bimodules equipped with the composition product based on 2-leveled connected graphs directed by a global flow, see for instance
[
HLV21
, Section 1]
for more details.

1
2
3
4
1
3
2
3
1
2
4

Figure 1.
Examples of 2-leveled connected graph directed from top to bottom.

Definition 1.1

(Properad and coproperad)
.

A
properad
(resp.
coproperad
) is a monoid (resp.
comonoid
) in the monoidal category

\big(\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}\mathsf{bimod},\allowbreak\boxtimes,\allowbreak\mathcal{I}\big)
.

Examples 1.2
.

\diamond

Endomorphism properad:

The
endomorphism properad
of a chain complex
A
is made up of all its multilinear maps:

\mathrm{End}_{A}\coloneq\left\{\mathrm{Hom}\left(A^{\otimes n},A^{{\otimes}m}\right)\right\}_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}\ .

\diamond

Convolution properad:

For any coproperad
\mathcal{C}
and any properad
\mathcal{P}
, the mapping

\mathbb{S}
-bimodule

\{\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{C}(m,n),\mathcal{P}(m,n)\}_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}

is endowed with a canonical natural properad structure called the
convolution properad
, see
[
MV09a
, Section 2.1]
.

Let us now recall various properties and constructions of properads and coproperads, see
[
HLV21
, Section 2]
for more details.
We denote the set of
connected graphs directed by a global flow
by
\mathsf{dcGra}
. By convention, we exclude the trivial graph
|
from living in
\mathsf{dcGra}
.
For any
\mathbb{S}
-bimodule
\mathcal{M}
, the
graph module

\mathrm{g}(\mathcal{M})
is the
\mathbb{S}
-module obtained by labelling each vertex of
\mathrm{g}
by an element of
\mathcal{M}
of corresponding arity.
By convention, we set
|(\mathcal{M})\coloneq\mathcal{I}
.
The notion of a properad is equivalently defined as an algebra over the monad of graphs

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{M})\coloneq\bigoplus_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dcGra}\cup\{|\}}\mathrm{g}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

whose structure product is given by the substitution of graphs.
For any
k\geqslant 1
, we denote by
\mathsf{dcGra}^{(k)}
the set of connected directed graphs with
k
vertices.
We consider the
infinitesimal composition product

\mathcal{M}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{M}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dcGra}^{(2)}}\mathrm{g}(\mathcal{M})

made up of labelled graphs with 2 vertices and an arbitrary number of edges connecting them.
The
infinitesimal composition map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{(1,1)}\ :\ \mathcal{P}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

of a properad
(\mathcal{P},\gamma,\eta)
amounts to composing only two operations at a time.

One way to get a single chain complex from an
\mathbb{S}
-bimodule is to consider the total sum of the invariant parts of its components:

\bigoplus_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(m,n)^{\mathbb{S}_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}}\penalty\ .

Recall that a binary product
\star
is called
Lie-admissible product
when its commutator

[\ ,\,]\coloneq\ \star\ -\ \star^{(12)}
satisfies the Jacobi identity.

Lemma 1.3

(
[
MV09a
, Proposition 6]
)
.

The infinitesimal composition map of a dg properad

(\mathcal{P},\allowbreak\mathrm{d},\allowbreak\gamma,\allowbreak\eta)

induces a dg Lie-admissible algebra structure

\left(\oplus_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(m,n)^{\mathbb{S}_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}},\mathrm{d},\star\right)

on
the total sum of the invariant parts of its components.

Unfortunately, the construction of the Lie-admissible product
\star
is not well defined on the total
product
of the components of a properad, since it might produce infinite sums of elements. For this to hold, we need to restrict ourselves to proper subclasses of properads, like the convolution properads
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
, for instance.

The properties of coproperads are not exactly dual to the properties of properads.
For instance, coalgebras over the graphs comonad

\mathcal{G}^{c}(\mathcal{M})\coloneq\bigoplus_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dcGra}}\mathrm{g}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

whose structure coproduct is given by the partitions of graphs,
do not encompass all coproperads: they
induce coaugmented coproperad structures that are called
conilpotent
, see
[
HLV21
, Proposition 2.18]
.
Given a coaugmented coproperad
\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
the cokernel of the coaugmentation map and we denote by

\widetilde{\Delta}\ :\ \overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{G}^{c}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\right)

the comonadic decomposition map of conilpotent coproperads.
The
infinitesimal decomposition map
of a coaugmented coproperad
(\mathcal{C},\Delta)
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{(1,1)}\ :\ \overline{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{C}}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

see
[
HLV21
, Definition 2.20]
for more details.

Definition 1.4

(Infinitesimal coproperad)
.

An
infinitesimal coproperad
is an
\mathbb{S}
-bimodule
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with a morphism of
\mathbb{S}
-bimodules

\Delta_{(1,1)}\colon\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\penalty\ ,

which satisfies the properties of the restriction of a comonadic product, see
[
MV09a
, Section 2]
.

Any coaugmented coproperad
\mathcal{C}
carries a canonical infinitesimal coproperad structure on its coaugmentation coideal
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
by composing its 2-levels decomposition map with the counit everywhere expect for one place above and one place below.
Any comonadic coproperad
\mathcal{C}
carries a canonical infinitesimal coproperad structure by restriction of the decomposition to directed graphs with two vertices. Any infinitesimal coproperad
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
can be coaugmented by adding freely a counit
\mathcal{C}\coloneq\overline{\mathcal{C}}\oplus\mathcal{I}
.
In the other way round, an infinitesimal coproperad might fail producing a coproperad or a comonadic coproperad by iterating its infinitesimal decomposition maps since this process might produce infinite sums of elements. When the image of any element under all the possible iterations of the infinitesimal decomposition map produces a finite sum, one gets a notion equivalent to a conilpotent coproperad. To summarise, under the conilpotent condition, the three notions of coproperad, comonadic coproperad, and infinitesimal coproperad are equivalent.

The main object of study of this work will be the following algebraic structure.

Proposition 1.5

(Convolution algebra
[
MV09a
, Proposition 11]
)
.

For any infinitesimal dg coproperad
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and any dg module
A
,
the data

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}:=\left(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right),\partial,\star\right)\ ,

where

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)\coloneq\prod_{n,m\geqslant 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}_{m}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}(m,n),\mathrm{Hom}\left(A^{{\otimes}n},A^{{\otimes}m}\right)\right)

and

f\star g\ :\ \overline{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{\Delta_{(1,1)}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{f\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}g}\mathrm{End}_{A}\underset{\scriptscriptstyle(1,1)}{\boxtimes}\mathrm{End}_{A}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{(1,1)}}\mathrm{End}_{A}\

forms a Lie-admissible algebra, called the
convolution algebra
.

When the infinitesimal coproperad comes from a coaugmented coproperad, the convolution Lie-admissible algebra
is contained into the bigger dg module

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)\subset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)\ ,

which admits the following two distinguished elements
\mathbb{1}
of degree
0
and
\delta
of degree
-1
defined respectively by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}\ :\ \mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}_{A}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\ :\ \mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\ ,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{A}\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{A}}

where
\varepsilon
stands for the counit of the coproperad
\mathcal{C}
and where
\partial_{A}
stands for the differential of
\mathrm{End}_{A}
.

Definition 1.6

(Convolution monoid)
.

The

convolution monoid

associated to any dg coproperad
\mathcal{C}

and any dg module
A
, is defined on the degree
0
elements of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)
by

{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{C},A}:=\big(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)_{0},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\big)}\ ,

equipped with the following associative and unital product

f\circledcirc g\ :\ \mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathcal{C}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{f\boxtimes g}\mathrm{End}_{A}\boxtimes\mathrm{End}_{A}\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathrm{End}_{A}\ ,

where
\Delta
and
\gamma
stand respectively for the decomposition map of the coproperad
\mathcal{C}
and the composition map of the endomorphism operad
\mathrm{End}_{A}
.

Definition 1.7

(Maurer–Cartan element)
.

A
Maurer–Cartan element
is a degree
-1
element
\bar{\alpha}
of a dg Lie-admissible algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathrm{d},\star)
satisfying the equation

\mathrm{d}\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\alpha}\star\bar{\alpha}=0\ .

We denote the set of Maurer–Cartan elements by
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Proposition 1.8

(
[
MV09a
, Proposition 17]
)
.

For any infinitesimal coaugmented dg coproperad
\overline{\mathcal{C}}

and any dg module
A
, the
Maurer–Cartan elements of the convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}

are in natural one-to-one correspondence with
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-gebra structures on
A
.

Example 1.9
.

The main range of applications for this result is when one considers the Koszul dual coproperad
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{P}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm!`}}
. In this case, Maurer–Cartan elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{P}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm!`}},A}
coincide with homotopy
\mathcal{P}
-gebra structures on
A
.

1.2.
Lie-graph algebras

The convolution algebra actually carries a more refined algebraic structure governed by a new operad that we introduce now.

Definition 1.10

(Directed simple graph)
.

We call
directed simple graph
a connected graph
\mathrm{g}
, directed by a global flow from top to bottom, with at least one vertex, with no input, no output and at most one edge between two vertices.
The number of vertices of a graph
\mathrm{g}
is denoted by
|\mathrm{g}|
and they are labeled bijectively by
1,\ldots,|\mathrm{g}|
. The set of directed simple graphs is denoted by
\mathsf{dsGra}
; examples are given in
Figure

2
.

1
2
1
1
2
3
1
2
3
3
2
1
3
2
1
1
2
3
4
1
2
3
4
5

Figure 2.
The first directed simple graphs.

The canonical action of the symmetric groups on the labels of the vertices of directed simple graphs endow the set
\mathsf{dsGra}

with a set-theoretical
\mathbb{S}
-module structure. We denote by
\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dsGra}
the
\mathbb{k}
-linear
\mathbb{S}
-module spanned by
\mathsf{dsGra}
. We endow it with the partial composition products
\mathrm{g}_{1}\circ_{i}\mathrm{g}_{2}
defined by the sum of graphs obtained by

(1)

inserting the graph
\mathrm{g}_{2}
at the vertex
i
of the graph
\mathrm{g}_{1}
,

(2)

for any edge joining a vertex
j
to the vertex
i
in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, considering the sum of all the possible
ways to join with edges the vertex
j
to at least one vertex of
\mathrm{g}_{2}
,

(3)

keeping the labels
1,\ldots,i-1
of the graph
\mathrm{g}_{1}
,

(4)

shifting the labels of
\mathrm{g}_{2}
by
i-1
,

(5)

and shifting the remaining vertices of
\mathrm{g}_{1}
by
|\mathrm{g}_{2}|-1
.

.

forms an algebraic operad.

Proof.

The element

1

is clearly a unit for the partial composition products. The parallel and sequential axioms are straightforward to check. The various compatibilities with the action of the symmetric groups are also automatic.
∎

Definition 1.12

(Lie-graph algebra)
.

We call
Lie-graph algebra
an algebra over the operad

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\coloneq(\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dsGra},\{\circ_{i}\},\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.81 0 L 11.81 11.81 L 0 11.81 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.56653pt}{2.65685pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})\penalty\ .

The terminology chosen here comes from the relationship with the following various Lie-type algebraic structures.
As we will see in
Proposition

1.20
and
Theorem

1.21
, this operad encodes all the natural operations acting on the totalisation of properad and thus on the deformation complex of morphisms of properads, which is necessary to adequately express the gauge group.
Contrary to operads such as
\mathrm{Lie}
,
\mathrm{Ass}
,
\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}
, which are finitely presented and easily described in terms of generators and relations, no such description is available for
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
. Informally, this means that we cannot bypass operad theory to even define
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
-algebras by a finite set of structural operations satisfying some relations. This can be formalised by the proposition below, whose proof we postpone to
Appendix

A
.

Proposition 1.13
.

The operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is not finitely generated.

First, the binary operation of
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
given by the graph with two vertices satisfies the Lie-admissible relation, i.e. we have a morphism of operads

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}&\to&\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\\
\star&\mapsto&\vbox{\hbox{
\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to35.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.86476pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 31.5 L 15.75 31.5 L 15.75 47.24 L 0 47.24 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 15.75 L 7.87 31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.19046pt}{2.46825pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.19046pt}{25.2301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

It is worth noticing that this morphism of operads
fails to be surjective
since for instance

\dim\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}(3)=11\neq\dim\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(3)=18\penalty\ .

This means that the data of a Lie-graph algebra structure is significantly richer than that of a Lie-admissible product.

The operation of skew-symmetrising a Lie-admissible product produces a Lie bracket; this amounts to a morphism of operads

\mathrm{Lie}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}
, which is injective after
[
MR06
]
. So, any Lie-graph algebra structure contains a Lie-admissible product which yields a Lie bracket under skew-symmetrisation; that induces the following composite of operadic morphisms:

\mathrm{Lie}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}\to\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\ .

The notion of a Lie-graph algebra generalises the main type of algebraic structure governing the operadic deformation theory: pre-Lie algebras. Let us recall that a pre-Lie product is a binary operation whose associator is right symmetric, see
[
LV12
, Section 13.4]
. The associated binary quadratic operad admits a graphical interpretation
[
CL01
]
: it is isomorphic to the operad of rooted trees

\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\cong\mathrm{RT}\penalty\ ,

where the operadic composition map is similar to the one of the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
(insertion at a vertex and then grafting), except that one does not allow the number of edges to increase, see
Figure

4
.

Example of a partial composition in the operad
\mathrm{RT}

In the same range of ideas, the operad
\mathrm{Ass}
governing associative algebras can also to described in a graphical way as follows.
We consider the subset of rooted trees made up of
ladders
, that is rooted trees such that every vertex has at most one input.
We endow this
\mathbb{S}
-set with the partial composition products
\circ_{i}
where one first inserts the second ladder at the place of the
i
-th vertex of the first ladder and where one then grafts the root vertex and the top vertex of the second ladder in order to produce a final ladder, see
Figure

5
.

Figure 5.
Example of a partial composition of ladders.

Denoting by
\mathrm{Lad}
the
\mathbb{k}
-linear operad spanned by the set of ladders, one gets the canonical isomorphism of operads

\mathrm{Ass}\cong\mathrm{Lad}\penalty\ .

The projection of rooted trees onto ladders induces a surjection of operads

\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\cong\mathrm{RT}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Lad}\cong\mathrm{Ass}\penalty\ ,

whose pullback coincides with the fact that an associative product gives a pre-Lie product. A similar relation holds between the operads
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
and
\mathrm{RT}
. Since all the edges of a directed simple graph are oriented, they induce a partial order on the set of vertices, where we say that a vertex at the source of an edge is greater than the vertex lying at the target of that edge.

Proposition 1.14
.

The projection of directed simple graphs onto the ones of genus 0 with only one minimum vertex defines an epimorphism of operads

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{RT}\cong\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\ .

Proof.

First we notice that the subset of genus 0 directed simple graphs with only one minimum vertex is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of rooted trees. In one way, rooted trees are directed simple graphs: for such trees, one orients the edges from the leaves to the root.
In the other way around, one can prove, for instance by induction on the number of vertices, that every vertex of a genus 0 directed simple graph with only one minimum vertex has at most one output edge.
Then one can see that the projection of directed simple graphs onto rooted trees preserves the respective operadic partial composition products: its kills the extra terms appearing in the partial composition product of the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
and not in the one of the operad
\mathrm{RT}
.
∎

It is worth noticing that the canonical inclusions of graphs in the other way round, that is rooted trees into directed simple graphs and ladders into rooted trees,
do not respect
the operadic structures and so they fail to define morphisms of operads. In the end, we get the following relations between these various algebraic structures:

\mathrm{Lie}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}\to\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Ass}\ ,

where
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is the biggest operad.

Remark 1.15
.

Despite an apparent similar flavour, the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is quite different from the graphs operads considered in the study of the little discs operad, the configuration spaces of points, and the Grothendieck–Teichmüller Lie algebra, see
[
Kon97
,
LV14
,
Wil15
,
Idr19
,
CW23
]
and the survey
[
DSV24
, Chapter 6]
: they all share the property that their composition preserves the number of edges.

More generally, we consider the set
\mathsf{dsncGra}
of
directed simple non-necessarily connected graphs
, see

Figure

6
for examples. We endow them with the partial composition products
\mathrm{g}_{1}\circ_{i}\mathrm{g}_{2}
defined similarly by the sum of graphs obtained by first inserting the graph
\mathrm{g}_{2}
at the vertex
i
of the graph
\mathrm{g}_{1}
,
then joining any vertex
j
of
\mathrm{g}_{1}
, attached previously to
i
, in all possible ways to vertices of
\mathrm{g}_{2}
with at least one edge each time,
and finally relabeling the vertices accordingly.

between the operads

\mathrm{Com}

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
, which is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dsncGra},\{\circ_{i}\},\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})
.

Proof.

Like in the proof of
Lemma

1.11
, the unit, parallel, sequential, and equivariance axioms are straightforward to prove.

Let us make fully explicit the distributive law. To any directed simple graph
\mathrm{g}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
with
k
vertices labelled bijectively by the components of a partition

\pi_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\pi_{k}

of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we associate the sum of directed simple non-necessarily connected graphs obtained by
replacing all the numbers of the components of the partition by a vertex labeled by that number, and by replacing the edges joining two vertices in the graph
\mathrm{g}
by the sum of way to join these new vertices with at least one edge each time:

The axioms
\mathrm{(I)}
,
\mathrm{(II)}
,
\mathrm{(i)}
, and,
\mathrm{(ii)}
, see
[
LV12
, Section 8.6.1]
, defining the notion of a distributive law are straightforward to check. Finally, it is easy to see that these two operads are isomorphic since the composite product
\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is given by disjoint unions of direct simple graphs and since the respective two composition maps are the same.
∎

Remark 1.17
.

Notice that, since the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is not defined by generators and relations, this distributive law is not induced by a rewriting rule, which is rather unusual.

Definition 1.18

(Lie-ncgraph algebra)
.

We call
Lie-ncgraph algebra
an algebra over the operad

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}\coloneq(\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dsncGra},\{\circ_{i}\},\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The inclusion
\mathsf{dsGra}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{dsncGra}
defines an embedding of operads
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
.

Remark 1.19
.

On the other hand, the natural projection
\mathsf{dsncGra}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{dsGra}
does not induce a morphism of operads, as the potential creation of edges means that inserting disconnected graphs can produce connected graphs. For the same reason, the commutative product and the Lie bracket do not satisfy the Leibniz relation and therefore the operad

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
does not contain the Poisson operad. This is in contrast with the graph operads mentioned in
Remark

1.15
.

1.3.
Lie-graph algebra structure on properadic convolution algebra

Conversely, one might wonder whether it is possible to lift Lie-admissible structures to Lie-graph algebra structures.
In the cases of the sum of the components of a properad and of properadic convolution algebras, we solve this question as follows.
We consider the
barber
map

Proposition 1.20
.

The total sum
\oplus_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(m,n)^{\mathbb{S}_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}}
of the invariant parts of the components of any dg properad
(\mathcal{P},\mathrm{d},\gamma,\eta)
carries a natural dg Lie-graph algebra structure

\mathrm{g}(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k})\coloneq\sum_{\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathsf{dcGra}\atop\mathrm{barb}(\widetilde{\mathrm{g}})=\mathrm{g}}\gamma\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}\left(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}\right)\big)\penalty\ ,

which extends the Lie-admissible product of
Lemma

1.3
.

Proof.

Let us first notice that the set of connected directed graphs
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}
satisfying
\mathrm{barb}(\widetilde{\mathrm{g}})=\mathrm{g}
is infinite but since each
\mu_{i}
is a finite linear combination, the output of

\mathrm{g}(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k})
is actually a finite sum, so it is well defined.
Since

\mathrm{barb}^{-1}(\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.81 0 L 11.81 11.81 L 0 11.81 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.56653pt}{2.65685pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})=\{c_{m,n}\}_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
c_{m,n}
stands for the 1-vertex graph with
n
inputs and
m
-outputs,
the 1-vertex graph

1

acts as the identity:

\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.81 0 L 11.81 11.81 L 0 11.81 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.56653pt}{2.65685pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}(\mu)=\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(m,n)=\mu\penalty\ .

For any pair
\mathrm{g}_{1},\mathrm{g}_{2}\in\mathsf{dsGra}
of directed simple graphs and for elements
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s+t-1}\in\allowbreak\oplus_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\allowbreak\mathcal{P}(m,n)^{\mathbb{S}_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{S}_{n}}
, where
s=|\mathrm{g}_{1}|
and
t=|\mathrm{g}_{2}|
, we have to show that

\mathrm{g}_{1}(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i-1},\mathrm{g}_{2}(\mu_{i},\ldots,\mu_{i+s-1}),\mu_{i+s},\ldots,\mu_{s+t-1})=(\mathrm{g}_{1}\circ_{i}\mathrm{g}_{2})(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s+t-1})\ .

The left-hand side is obtained by first applying the monadic properad structure on
\mu_{i},\ldots,\mu_{i+s-1}
along all the directed connected graphs
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}_{2}
of barber type
\mathrm{g}_{2}
and then applying the same procedure to the result
and to
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i-1},\mu_{i},\ldots,\mu_{i+s-1}
along all the
directed connected graphs
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}_{1}
of barber type
\mathrm{g}_{1}
.
The set of directed connected graphs that appear here are the ones of barber type
\mathrm{g}_{1}\circ_{i}\mathrm{g}_{2}
by the definition of the partial composition products of the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
.
The axiom defining the algebra structure of the properad
\mathcal{P}
over the graph monad
\mathfrak{G}
concludes this part of the proof.

The induced Lie-admissible product is given by the action of the 2-vertices graph

2

1

, which is equal to the product
\star
defined in
Lemma

1.3
.
∎

Theorem 1.21
.

The convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
associated to an infinitesimal dg coproperad
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and a dg module
A
carries the following natural dg Lie-graph algebra structure

\mathrm{g}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})\ :\ \overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{G}^{c}\big(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\big)\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathsf{dcGra}^{(k)}\atop\mathrm{barb}(\widetilde{\mathrm{g}})=\mathrm{g}}\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}\big(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\big)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})}\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{End}_{A})\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathrm{End}_{A}\ ,

where
k=|\mathrm{g}|
, which extends the Lie-admissible product of
Proposition

1.5
.

The map
\gamma
stands for the monadic composition map of the properad
\mathrm{End}_{A}
and
the maps
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}\big(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\big)
are suitable iterations of the infinitesimal decomposition maps of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.
The notation
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
means that we apply
f_{i}:\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{End}_{A}
to the label of the vertex
i
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}
, for any
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Proof.

The computations are similar to that of
Proposition

1.20
.
Given any directed simple graph
\mathrm{g}
, all the ways to iterate the infinitesimal decomposition maps of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
which produce graphs of type
\widetilde{\mathrm{g}}
, such that
\mathrm{barb}(\widetilde{\mathrm{g}})=\mathrm{g}
, are equal by the axioms of an infinitesimal coproperad, and they produce finite sums, once applied to elements

c\in\mathcal{C}(m,n)
.
Therefore the action
\mathrm{g}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
of the Lie-graph operations is well defined.
∎

Remark 1.22
.

If we were developing the same theory with associative algebras, that is with properads concentrated in arity
(1,1)
, we would first get the structure of an associative algebra on the convolution algebra between an associative coalgebra and an a coassociative coalgebra.
This would be enough to write down the Maurer–Cartan equation. Then, the potentially bigger algebraic structure would here be that of an algebra encoded by the ladders operad. But in this case, both are isomorphic
\mathrm{Ass}\cong\mathrm{Lad}
, and one would not get more structure.

If we were working with operads, that is with properads concentrated in arities
(1,n)
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we would get
first the operad of pre-Lie algebras and then the operad of rooted trees. But, again, they turn out to be isomorphic

\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\cong\mathrm{RT}
, by
[
CL01
]
. So we would not get any additional structure in this case either.

In the present case of properads, there is significant gap between the minimal structure needed to write the Maurer–Cartan equation and the maximal structure made up of all the operations acting on the deformation complex: recall from
Section

1.2
that the morphism of operads
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}\to\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
fails to be surjective. Thus the Lie-graph algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}

cannot
be obtained by iterating its Lie-admissible product. This key remark conceptually explains the discrepancy between the properadic calculus developed below and the operadic calculus developed in
[
DSV16
]
.

A similar phenomenon can be observed on the level of modular operads, see
[
DSVV24
]
, where the operad of connected graphs
\mathrm{CGra}
is the operad of all the natural operations acting on the totalisation of a modular operad while the operad
\Delta\mathrm{Lie}
encodes the minimal structure required to write down the Maurer–Cartan equation.
The totalisation of any dg prop, admits a Lie-admissible structure, via the usual vertical composition formula, which extends to a canonical
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
-algebra structure. The first classical example is the
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
-structure is given by the Weyl algebra
S(V\oplus V^{*})
, see
[
BV25
, Section 1]
for instance, which is actually the totalisation of the endomorphism prop of the chain complex
V
. We refer the reader to
Figure

7
for survey of these various cases.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\cline{2-3}\cr\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\textsc{minimal structure}&\textsc{maximal structure}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\textsc{algebras}&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\mathrm{Ass}&\mathrm{Ass}\cong\mathrm{Lad}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\textsc{operads}&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}&\mathrm{pre}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Lie}\cong\mathrm{RT}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\textsc{modular operads}&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\Delta\mathrm{Lie}&\mathrm{CGra}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\textsc{properads}&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}&\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\textsc{props}&\rule{0.0pt}{10.0pt}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{adm}&\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\end{array}

Figure 7.
The various algebraic structures present in the operadic deformation theory.

1.4.
The complete setting

Like the classical integration theory of Lie algebras, the integration theory of Lie-graph algebras, that we will develop in the next section
2
, will require to consider infinite series of elements. In order to control their behavior, we need complete topologies on the underlying chain complexes.

Definition 1.23

(Complete differential graded module)
.

A
complete differential graded (dg) module

(A,\mathrm{F},\mathrm{d})
is a chain complex in the category of complete
\mathbb{k}
-modules. In other words, each degreewise component is equipped with a descending filtration

A_{n}=\mathrm{F}_{0}A_{n}\supset\mathrm{F}_{1}A_{n}\supset\mathrm{F}_{2}A_{n}\supset\cdots\supset\mathrm{F}_{k}A_{n}\supset\mathrm{F}_{k+1}A_{n}\supset\cdots

made up of submodules satisfying
\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{F}_{k}A_{n})\subset\mathrm{F}_{k}A_{n-1}
and
which is complete. That is, the canonical map

A\xrightarrow{\cong}\widehat{A}\coloneq\varprojlim_{k}A/\mathrm{F}_{k}A

is an isomorphism.

We refer the reader for instance to
[
DSV24
, Section 2]
for a detailed exposition of the properties of complete differential graded modules.
Let us quickly recall the main ones used throughout this text.
The internal Hom structure is given by

\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)\coloneq\big\{f:A\to B\ |\ f(\mathrm{F}_{l}A)\subset\mathrm{F}_{k+l}B\ ,\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}\big\}\ .

We denote with small letters the subspace of maps which preserve the respective filtrations:

\hom(A,B)\coloneq\mathrm{F}_{0}\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)\ .

The induced filtration on the tensor product of two filtered modules is given by

{\mathrm{F}}_{k}(A\otimes B)\coloneq\sum_{j+l=k}\mathrm{Im}\big(\mathrm{F}_{j}A\otimes\mathrm{G}_{l}B\to A\otimes B\big)

and their complete tensor product is defined by

A\widehat{\otimes}B\coloneq\widehat{A\otimes B}\ .

Lemma 1.24

(
[
DSV24
, Chapter 2, Lemma 2.19]
)
.

The category of complete dg modules forms a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category, whose monoidal product preserves colimits.

This allows one to perform operadic and properadic calculus in this setting, see
[
DSV24
, Chapter 2]
and
[
HLV21
, Sections 1-2]
. For instance a
complete
dg Lie-graph algebra is a complete dg module

(A,\mathrm{F},\mathrm{d})
equipped with operations

\mathrm{g}\ :\ A^{\otimes|\mathrm{g}|}\to A\penalty\ ,

for any directed simple graph
\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}
, satisfying the axioms of a dg Lie-graph algebra and preserving the filtration, that is

\mathrm{g}\ :\ \mathrm{F}_{i_{1}}A{\otimes}\cdots{\otimes}\mathrm{F}_{i_{k}}A\to\mathrm{F}_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}}A\penalty\ .

Actually, the convolution algebras associated to conilpotent coproperads always admit a compatible complete filtration as follows.
We denote by

\mathcal{G}^{c}(\mathcal{M})^{(k)}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dcGra}^{(k)}}\mathrm{g}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

the summand of the graphs comonad spanned by directed connected graphs with
k
vertices.

Definition 1.25

(Coradical filtration)
.

The
coradical filtration
of the coaugmentation coideal
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
of a conilpotent coproperad is defined by

\mathscr{R}_{k}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\coloneq\left\{c\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}\ |\ \widetilde{\Delta}(c)\in\bigoplus_{l=1}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{c}\Big(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\Big)^{(l)}\right\}\penalty\ .

By definition, this filtration is increasing and exhaustive:

0=\mathscr{R}_{0}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathscr{R}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathscr{R}_{2}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subset\cdots\subset\mathscr{R}_{k}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathscr{R}_{k+1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subset\cdots\quad\&\quad\bigcup_{k\geqslant 1}\mathscr{R}_{k}\overline{\mathcal{C}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}}\penalty\ .

The first non-trivial part of the coradical filtration is the space of primitive elements:

\mathscr{R}_{1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{c\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}\ |\ \widetilde{\Delta}(c)=c\right\}\penalty\ .

Definition 1.26

(Canonical filtration)
.

The
canonical filtration
of the convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
associated to a conilpotent dg coproperad
\mathcal{C}
and a dg module
A
is defined by

\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\coloneq\left\{f:\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{End}_{A}\ ;\ f|_{\mathscr{R}_{k-1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}}=0\right\}\penalty\ .

Proposition 1.27
.

For any conilpotent dg coproperad
\mathcal{C}
and any dg module
A
, the convolution algebra

\left(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A},\mathrm{F},\partial,\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}}\right)\penalty\ ,

equipped with the canonical filtration,
forms a complete dg Lie-graph algebra.

Proof.

First, the canonical filtration is obviously decreasing

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}=\mathrm{F}_{0}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}=\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\supset\mathrm{F}_{2}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\supset\cdots\supset\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\subset\mathrm{F}_{k+1}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\supset\cdots

and, since the coradical filtration is exhaustive, the canonical filtration is complete.
Then, since the underlying differential
d_{\overline{\mathcal{C}}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is a coderivation with respect to the graphs comonadic coalgebra structure, it preserves the coradical filtration. This implies that the differential

\partial f=\partial_{A}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d_{\overline{\mathcal{C}}}

of the convolution algebra
preserves the canonical filtration.
Finally, let us show that the operations of the Lie-graph algebra structure preserve the canonical filtration.
For any
k\geqslant 1
, let
\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}^{(k)}
and let

f_{j}\in\mathrm{F}_{i_{j}}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
, for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
.
We have to show that
\mathrm{g}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})\in\mathrm{F}_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
, that is

\mathrm{g}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})|_{\mathscr{R}_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}}=0
.
For any
c\in\mathscr{R}_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, the projection of
\widetilde{\Delta}(c)
onto
\mathcal{G}^{c}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{(k)}
is a sum of graphs with vertices labelled by elements of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
such that at least one of them lives in
\mathscr{R}_{i_{j}-1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, for
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, by the coassociativity of the graphs comonad: otherwise
\widetilde{\Delta}(c)
would have one non-trivial term in
\mathcal{G}^{c}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}\right)^{(i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k})}
, which would contradict
c\in\mathscr{R}_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. Thus we get
\mathrm{g}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})(c)=0
.
∎

One can suppress the conilpotency assumption of
Proposition

1.27
at the cost of requiring completeness from the dg module
A
. This is a pertinent assumption to drop, already in the operadic context, in which the deformation complex encoding curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A

is provided by the convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathsf{uCom^{*}},A}
associated to the infinitesimal cooperad
\mathsf{uCom^{*}}
of counital cocommutative coalgebras which fails to be conilpotent, see
[
DSV16
, Section 4.3]
and
[
CCN21
]
for instance.
This point is crucial for the twisting procedure described in
[
CV25
, Section 4]
. We denote
by
\hom
the filtered space of continuous maps and
by
\mathrm{end}_{A}
the complete endomorphism properad made up of continuous maps.

Proposition 1.28
.

For any filtered infinitesimal dg coproperad
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and any complete dg module
A
, the convolution algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\coloneq\left(\hom_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{end}_{A}\right),\mathrm{F},\partial,\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}}\right)\penalty\ ,

equipped with the filtration induced by
A
,
forms a complete dg Lie-graph algebra.

Proof.

The formulas and the arguments
Theorem

1.21
still apply in the complete setting: the Lie-graph operations are well defined and they preserve the respective filtrations.
∎

Let us conclude this section with a variant of the Lie-graph operad which takes into account the numbers of edges between vertices and
which is complete.

Definition 1.29

(Directed multiple-edges graph)
.

A
directed multiple-edges graph
is a connected graph
\mathrm{g}
, directed by a global flow from top to bottom, with at least one vertex, with no input, no output and an arbitrary number of edges between two vertices.
We denote their set by
\mathsf{dmGra}
.

1
2
3
4

Figure 8.
An example of a directed multiple-edges graph.

On the
\mathbb{k}
-linear
\mathbb{S}
-module
\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dmGra}
spanned by
\mathsf{dmGra}
, we consider
the filtration defined by the number of edges:

\mathscr{F}_{k}\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dmGra}\coloneq\bigoplus_{e\geqslant k}\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dmGra}_{[e]}\penalty\ ,

where
\mathsf{dmGra}_{[e]}
stands for the set of directed multiple-edges graphs with
e
edges.
We consider partial composition products
\mathrm{g}_{1}\circ_{i}\mathrm{g}_{2}
defined in the same way as for the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
replacing Point (2) by:

(2’)

for any edge joining a vertex
j
to the vertex
i
in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, considering the sum of all the possible
ways to join with one edge the vertex
j
to one vertex of
\mathrm{g}_{2}
.

So this time, the number of edges is preserved.

Lemma 1.30
.

The data
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}\coloneq(\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dmGra},\mathscr{F},\{\circ_{i}\},\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

forms a filtered algebraic operad.

Proof.

The axioms are straightforward to check, like in the proof of
Lemma

1.11
.
∎

Definition 1.31

(Complete Lie-mgraph operad)
.

The
complete operad
\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}}
is defined as the completion of the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}
:

\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}}\coloneq\left(\widehat{\mathbb{k}\mathsf{dmGra}},\mathscr{F},\{\circ_{i}\},\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.07pt{\vbox to9.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.76648pt\lower-0.67616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.81 0 L 11.81 11.81 L 0 11.81 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.56653pt}{2.65685pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\right)\penalty\ .

Elements of
\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}}(n)
are series of linear combination labelled by the number of edges
of directed multiple-edges graphs with
n
vertices.
There is an injective morphism of operads

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\rightarrowtail\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}}

given by assigning a directed simple graph
\mathrm{g}
to the sum of all directed multiple-edges graphs with the same vertices as
\mathrm{g}
and an arbitrary positive number of edges between vertices
i
and
j
, whenever there was an edge between
i
and
j
in
\mathrm{g}
.

Remark 1.32
.

The complete operad
\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{mgra}}(n)
is similar to the graphs operads considered in the study of the little discs operad, the configuration spaces of points, and the Grothendieck–Teichmüller Lie algebra
[
Kon97
,
LV14
,
Wil15
,
Idr19
,
CW23
,
DSV24
]
since its composition maps preserve the number of edges. One can consider a version of it where internal edges receive a certain degree.

2.
Integration theory of Lie-graph algebras

The Lie bracket of any complete Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, like the one associated the complete convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
, induces the following usual Hausdorff type group. Recall that the
Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula

is the element in the free associative algebra on two generators
x
and
y
, that is the algebra of formal power series on
x
and
y
,
given by

\mathrm{BCH}(x,y)\coloneq\mathrm{log}\left(e^{x}e^{y}\right)\penalty\ .

The celebrated theorem of Baker, Campbell, and Hausdorff, see
[
BF12
]
, states that this formula can be written using only the Lie bracket
[a,b]=a\otimes b-b\otimes a
:

\mathrm{BCH}(x,y)=x+y+\tfrac{1}{2}[x,y]+\tfrac{1}{12}[x,[x,y]]+\tfrac{1}{12}[y,[x,y]]+\cdots\penalty\ .\\

In other words, the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula
\mathrm{BCH}(x,y)
lives in the free complete Lie algebra on two generators.

Definition 2.1

(Gauge group)
.

The
gauge group
associated to a complete Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\{\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}\}_{k\geqslant 0},[\ ,\,])
is the complete group
defined by

\Gamma\coloneq\big(\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\,\{\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\}_{k\geqslant 1},\,\mathrm{BCH},\,0\big)\ ,

We refer the reader to
[
DSV24
, Definition 3.8]
for the notion of a complete group, which is a group equipped with a compatible and complete filtration. Since the
\mathrm{BCH}
formula is quite involved, one would appreciate to be able to use another more amenable form for this gauge group.
When the Lie bracket comes from a pre-Lie product, the BCH formula is actually given by a canonical closed formula based on 2-leveled rooted trees, see
[
DSV16
]
and
[
DSV24
, Chapter 3]
in the complete case.

The purpose of this section is to simplify the
\mathrm{BCH}
-formula for Lie algebras arising from the particular structure of a
Lie-graph algebra
. To this extent, we first introduce a deformation gauge group, which, in the case of the convolution algebra, is actually made up of the elements of the convolution monoid

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{C},A}
whose first component is the identity of
A
. Then, we coin a new exponential map, based on directed simple graphs, that provides us with an isomorphism between the gauge group and the deformation gauge group. Finally, we conclude this section with a closed graphical formula for their action on Maurer–Cartan elements.

2.1.
Deformation gauge group

Definition 2.2

(
k
-leveled directed simple graphs)
.

For any
k\geqslant 1
, we consider the set
k\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
of

k
-leveled directed simple graphs
, which are
directed simple graphs with the data of
k
levels, along which sit the unlabelled vertices. The orientation of the graph should respect strictly the order of the levels: when one passes from an upper vertex to a lower vertex along one edge then one should go from an upper level to a strictly lower level. One vertex can sit on only one level and empty levels are allowed.

Figure 9.
Three different
3
-leveled graphs.

Definition 2.3

(Automorphisms of
k
-leveled directed simple graphs)
.

The
automorphism group
of a
k
-leveled directed simple graphs
\mathrm{g}
is the group of permutations of vertices preserving both the adjacency condition and the level structure. We denote it by
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})
.

Example 2.4
.

In the three cases depicted on
Figure

9
, the automorphism groups are respective isomorphic to
\mathbb{S}_{2}
,
\mathbb{S}_{2}
, and
\mathbb{S}_{3}
.

Definition 2.5

(
\circledcirc
-product)
.

Given any complete Lie-graph algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathrm{F},\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}})
, we consider the set

\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\coloneq\{\mathbb{1}+x,\,x\in\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\}\penalty\ ,

where
\mathbb{1}
is a formal symbol.
We equip it with the following product

(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)\coloneq\mathbb{1}+\ \sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{2\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,y)\ ,

where
\mathrm{g}(x,y)
stands for the Lie-graph action of
\mathrm{g}
with bottom vertices labelled by
x
and top vertices labelled by
y
, see
Figure

10
.

(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)\ =\ \mathbb{1}\ +\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to12.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.50018pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Figure 10.
The first terms of the product
\circledcirc
.

Theorem 2.6
.

The above assignment defines a functor from complete Lie-graph algebras to complete groups:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\textrm{complete}\ \textrm{Lie-graph algebras}&\to&\textrm{complete groups}\\
\left(\mathfrak{g},\{\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}\}_{k\geqslant 0},\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}}\right)&\mapsto&\left(\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\{\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\}_{k\geqslant 1},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\right)\ .\end{array}

Proof.

We will show that, for the free complete Lie-graph algebra on three generators
x,y,z
, the following identity holds

(
\ast
)

((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z)=\mathbb{1}+\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in 3\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,y,z)\penalty\ ,

where
\mathrm{g}(x,y,z)
denotes the action of
\mathrm{g}
with all the vertices of the top level labelled by
z
, all the vertices of the middle level labelled by
y
, and all the vertices of the bottom level labelled by
x
.
The symmetry of the argument gives the same formula for the other parenthesization which guarantees the associativity of
\circledcirc
.

Since the operadic structure on
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is given by the insertion of directed graphs, we claim, from the definition of the product
\circledcirc
given in terms of 2-leveled graphs, that we can express its right iteration
using graphs with three levels, that is

((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z)=\mathbb{1}+\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in 3\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}\chi_{\mathrm{g}}\ \mathrm{g}(x,y,z)

for some coefficients
\chi_{\mathrm{g}}
that we wish to compute. Indeed the left-hand side is made up of terms of the form

(\cdots((\mathrm{g}^{\prime}\circ_{r}\mathrm{g}_{r})\circ_{r-1}\mathrm{g}_{r-1})\cdots)\circ_{1}\mathrm{g}_{1}
, with
\mathrm{g}_{i}
being
2
-leveled graphs in
x
and
y
, and
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
being a
2
-leveled graph with top level vertices labelled by
z
and bottom level vertices labelled by
1,\dots,r
. Such terms have an obvious 3-leveled structure.

In the other way round, let us fix a
3
-leveled graph
\mathrm{g}\in 3\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
. We start by noticing that for
\mathrm{g}
to appear as a summand in
(\cdots((\mathrm{g}^{\prime}\circ_{r}\mathrm{g}_{r})\circ_{r-1}\mathrm{g}_{r-1})\cdots)\circ_{1}\mathrm{g}_{1}
, both the graph
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
and the set of subgraphs
\{\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}\}
(accounting for multiplicities) are automatically determined. The latter graphs are obtained, up to permutation, by cutting out the top level of
\mathrm{g}
and the former graph
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathrm{g}
by contracting each sub-graph
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
into a vertex and by
deleting parallel edges, see
Figure

11
for an example.

Figure 11.
Example of a decomposition of 3-leveled graph.

One can actually say a bit more about the subgraphs
\mathrm{g}_{i}
of
\mathrm{g}
: they are actually more determined
when one considers the incident edges, called
leaves
of
\mathrm{g}_{i}
, connecting each of them to a top vertice labelled by
z
. Such graphs are called
marked
graphs: they are
2
-leveled directed simple graphs with at least one leaf, that is an edge going upward with no end vertex, and such that the leaves are colored according to the vertices they are attached to, see
Figure

12
.
Let us denote by
\mathrm{Aut}^{\text{mark}}
the group of marked automorphisms that
is, graph automorphisms that
respect the configuration of colored leaves.

Figure 12.
Example of a marked graph associated to the graph
\mathrm{g}
.

Let us suppose that there are
l
different types of marked graphs appearing among the
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
in
\mathrm{g}
and let us denote them by
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
, for
m=1,\dots,l
. Suppose furthermore that each marked graph
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
appears
j_{m}
times in
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
, so that
j_{1}+\dots+j_{l}=r
.
One can regroup them even further as follows: consider the various configurations
\mathrm{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{c}_{k}
of colors of the leaves with multiplicity of the marked graphs once one forgets the internal graph structure. For any
1\leqslant h\leqslant k
, we denote by
t_{h}
the number of marked graphs appearing among the
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
of colored leaves of configuration
\mathrm{c}_{h}
, so that
t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k}=r
is a coarser partition of
r
.

Any automorphism of the 3-leveled graph
\mathrm{g}
gives rise to an automorphism of the 2-leveled graph
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
, but this latter one fails to distinguish the
t_{1}
(resp.
t_{2},\ldots,t_{k}
) marked graphs of colored leaves of type
\mathrm{c}_{1}
(resp.
\mathrm{c}_{2},\ldots,\mathrm{c}_{k}
). Such an automorphism of
\mathrm{g}
also permutes identical marked subgraphs and applies to each of them a marked automorphism. In the other way round, this procedure fully characterises automorphisms of 3-leveled graphs, so we get

|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|=\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g}^{\prime})|\prod_{m=1}^{l}j_{m}!\,\left|\mathrm{Aut}^{\text{mark}}\left(\dot{\gamma}_{m}\right)\right|^{j_{m}}}{t_{1}!\ldots t_{k}!}\penalty\ .

Let us now compute the coefficient
\chi_{\gamma}
: there are 4 different coefficients.

i)

The coefficient of
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
, that is

\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g}^{\prime})|}\penalty\ .

ii)

The coefficient of the subgraphs
\mathrm{g}_{i}
which is equal to

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g}_{i})|}=\prod_{m=1}^{l}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma_{m})|^{j_{m}}}\penalty\ ,

where the
\gamma_{m}
are the underlying 2-leveled graphs of the marked graphs
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
obtained by removing the leaves.

iii)

A multinomial coefficient corresponding to the places where the marked graphs can be put.
For each
1\leqslant h\leqslant k
, we denote by
j_{m^{h}_{1}},\ldots,j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}
the respective numbers of marked graphs that have a configuration of colored leaves of type
\mathrm{c}_{h}
, so that

j_{m^{h}_{1}}+\cdots+j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}=t_{h}
and
\left\{m^{1}_{1},\ldots,m^{k}_{n(k)}\right\}=\{1,\ldots,l\}
.
On the
t_{h}
bottom vertices of
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}
, there are

\frac{t_{h}!}{j_{m^{h}_{1}}!\ldots j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}!}

ways to place the various marked graphs of type
\mathrm{c}_{h}
. In the end, this produces the coefficient

\prod_{h=1}^{k}\frac{t_{h}!}{j_{m^{h}_{1}}!\ldots j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}!}=\frac{t_{1}!\dots t_{k}!}{j_{1}!\dots j_{l}!}\penalty\ .

iv)

A
reconnection
coefficient
\mathrm{R}\left(\dot{\gamma}_{m}\right)
for each marked subgraph
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
, corresponding to different reconnections that yield the same final 3-leveled graph
\gamma
, see
Figure

13
for an example. A straightforward application of the orbit-stabiliser theorem shows that

\mathrm{R}\left(\dot{\gamma}_{m}\right)=\frac{\left|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma_{m})\right|}{\left|\mathrm{Aut}^{\text{mark}}\left(\dot{\gamma}_{m}\right)\right|}\penalty\ .

Figure 13.
These two graphs are identical but they appear twice in the
\circledcirc
product.

Multiplying all the coefficients, we get

\chi_{\gamma}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g}^{\prime})|}}_{\text{i)}}\times\underbrace{\prod_{m=1}^{l}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma_{m})|^{j_{m}}}}_{\text{ii)}}\times\underbrace{\frac{t_{1}!\dots t_{k}!}{j_{1}!\dots j_{l}!}}_{\text{iii)}}\times\underbrace{\prod_{m=1}^{l}\frac{\left|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma_{m})\right|^{j_{m}}}{\left|\mathrm{Aut}^{\text{mark}}\left(\dot{\gamma}_{m}\right)\right|^{j_{m}}}}_{\text{iv)}}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\penalty\ .

It remains to show that every element admits an inverse, which can be easily done by an inductive argument on the number of vertices. We skip this part of the proof since we will provide the reader with an explicit formula for the inverse in the following proposition.
∎

In a way similar to
Definition

2.2
, we consider the set
\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
of directed simple graphs with unlabelled vertices and no level structure this time. The group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})
of automorphisms of such a graph
\mathrm{g}
is made up of permutations of vertices which respect the adjacency condition.

Proposition 2.7
.

The inverse of an element
\mathbb{1}+x
in the complete group associated to a complete Lie-graph algebra is given by

(\mathbb{1}+x)^{-1}=\mathbb{1}+\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}\frac{(-1)^{|\mathrm{g}|}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\,\mathrm{g}(x)\penalty\ .

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.59468pt}{26.29955pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.83282pt}{3.53769pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
