{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.59468pt}{3.53769pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ +\ \cdots

Figure 14.
The first terms of the inverse.

Proof.

We use the same method as in the previous proof: we work in the
free complete Lie-graph algebra on one generator
x
, where we show that

(
\circledast
)

\left(\displaystyle\mathbb{1}+\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}\frac{(-1)^{|\mathrm{g}|}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\,\mathrm{g}(x)\right)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+x)=\mathbb{1}\penalty\ .

Expanding the left-hand side of
Equation

⊛

, one can see that it is equal to
\mathbb{1}+\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}\chi_{\mathrm{g}}\,\mathrm{g}(x)
, for some coefficients
\chi_{\mathrm{g}}
that we need to compute.

Let us fix a graph
\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
and let us denote by
T
its non-empty set of top vertices, i.e. vertices with no incoming edges.
Notice that the left-hand side of
Equation

⊛

is computed by picking graphs in
x
and adding top vertices, labeled again by
x
, along a 2-leveled graph.
In other words, for every subset
S\subset T
of top vertices of
\mathrm{g}
, the term
\mathrm{g}(x)
will appear as a summand of the form
(\cdots((\mathrm{g}^{\prime}\circ_{r}\mathrm{g}_{r})\circ_{r-1}\mathrm{g}_{r-1})\cdots)\circ_{1}\mathrm{g}_{1}
, where the
\mathrm{g}_{i}
are graphs in
\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
obtained from
\mathrm{g}
after deleting the vertices in
S
and where
\mathrm{g}^{\prime}\in 2\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}
is the 2-leveled graph obtained by taking the top vertices to be the ones of
S
, by contracting each of the graphs
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
into bottom vertices, and by merging parallel edges.

Let us denote by
\chi_{S}
the coefficient associated to graph
\mathrm{g}(x)
appearing in this way.
The computation of this coefficient is similar to the computation performed in the above proof of
Theorem

2.6
: one has just to consider
marked graphs
non-necessarily leveled together with their canonical notion of automorphisms. This way, we get

|\chi_{S}|=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\ .

This allows us to conclude the computation of the coefficient
\chi_{\mathrm{g}}
:

\chi_{\mathrm{g}}=\sum_{S\subset T}\chi_{S}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\sum_{S\subset T}(-1)^{|\mathrm{g}|-|S|}=0\penalty\ .

∎

Definition 2.8

(Deformation gauge group)
.

We call
deformation gauge group
associated to any complete Lie-graph algebra, the complete group

\mathfrak{G}\coloneq\big(\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\{\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\}_{k\geqslant 1},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\big)\penalty\ .

Remark 2.9
.

This group generalises the “group of formal flows” of
[
AG80
]
associated to a complete pre-Lie algebra and used in numerical analysis
[
Bro00
,
CHV10
,
LMK15
]
, renormalisation theory
[
CEFM11
,
BHZ19
]
, and geometry
[
Ban16
]
, for instance.
More precisely, the summand of the above formulae made up of rooted trees only gives the group associated to a complete pre-Lie algebra described in
[
DSV16
]
.

In the case of the convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
, the deformation gauge group, denoted by
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathcal{C},A}
,
actually sits inside the convolution monoid.

Proposition 2.10
.

The deformation gauge group of the convolution algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}
is isomorphic to the following complete sub-group of the convolution monoid
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{C},A}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\mathcal{C},A}\cong\left(\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)_{0},\left\{\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)_{0}\right\}_{k\geqslant 1},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\right)\subset\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{C},A}\penalty\ .

Proof.

The underlying space of the deformation gauge group is given by

\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{C},A}\right)_{0}=\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)_{0}\penalty\ .

Since the coproperad
\mathcal{C}
is coaugmented, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{End}_{A})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{End}_{A})\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{A}\right)\penalty\ .

Using the actual element
\mathbb{1}\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{C},A}
defined by sending
\mathrm{id}\in\mathcal{I}
to
\mathrm{id}_{A}
and zero elsewhere, we get the underlying isomorphism. It remains to check that the group product given by
Theorem

2.6
on this example coincides with
the associative product of
Definition

1.6
: given
f_{1},f_{2}:\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{End}_{A}
, we indeed have

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}+\ \sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{2\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsGra}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(f_{1},f_{2})\ :\ \mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathcal{C}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}}\mathrm{End}_{A}\boxtimes\mathrm{End}_{A}\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathrm{End}_{A}\penalty\ ,

by
Theorem

1.21
.
∎

A digression on the non-connected case

Motivated by the study of props, see
Section

1.3
and
Figure

7
, one is led to try to generalise the results of this section to the non-connected setting.
Given a complete Lie-ncgraph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we equip
\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
with the analogous product

(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+y)\coloneq\mathbb{1}+\ \sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{2\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsncGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,y)\ ,

where the sum runs over
2
-leveled non-necessarily connected graphs this time.
Unfortunately, this product cannot give rise to any meaningful “deformation gauge group” as it is not associative, see the following counterexample.

Counterexample 2.11
.

On the free complete Lie-ncgraph algebra on three generators
x,y,z
, the graph

z

z

z

y

x

x

appears with coefficient
\frac{5}{4}
in
((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+z)
and
appears with coefficient
\frac{1}{2}
in
(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}((\mathbb{1}+y)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+z))
.

On the other hand, there is a variation of associativity that is satisfied by
\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}
together with
\circledcirc
, as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 2.12
.

Given a complete
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
-algebra, we have

((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+z)=(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}((\mathbb{1}+y)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z))=\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in 3\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsncGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,y,z)\penalty\ ,

where the sum runs over
3
-leveled non-necessarily connected graphs.

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition

1.16
that the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
is given by a distributive law, so

\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}\cong\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
.
For
n\geqslant 1
, let
m_{n}
be the generator of
\mathrm{Com}(n)
. We start by showing that

(
\circleddash
)

(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+y)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}m_{n}\left(\big((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)\big)^{\otimes n}\right)\penalty\ .

Let us focus on the coefficient of a graph
\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsncGra}
. We write

\mathrm{g}=\big(\mathrm{g}^{1}_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup\mathrm{g}_{i_{1}}^{1}\big)\sqcup\dots\sqcup\big(\mathrm{g}_{1}^{k}\sqcup\dots\sqcup\mathrm{g}_{i_{k}}^{k}\big)\penalty\ ,

where
\mathrm{g}^{j}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{i_{j}}^{j}
are isomorphic connected components, whose isomorphism class will by denoted by
\mathrm{g}^{j}
. An automorphism of
\mathrm{g}
is given by automorphism of its connected components and permutations of isomorphic connected components, from which we deduce

|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|=\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{1}\big)\right|^{i_{1}}\dots\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{k}\big)\right|^{i_{k}}i_{1}!\dots i_{k}!\penalty\ .

The contributions of
\mathrm{g}
on the right-hand side of
Equation

⊝

can only come when we take
m_{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}}
of any permutation of
i_{1}
times the graph
\mathrm{g}^{1}
, …, and
i_{k}
times the graph
\mathrm{g}^{k}
. There are
\binom{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}}{i_{i},\dots,i_{k}}
of such permutations and therefore the total contribution of the right-hand side is equal to

\frac{1}{n!}\frac{1}{\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{1}\big)\right|^{i_{1}}\dots\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{k}\big)\right|^{i_{k}}}\binom{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}}{i_{i},\dots,i_{k}}=\frac{1}{\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{1}\big)\right|^{i_{1}}\dots\left|\mathrm{Aut}\big(\mathrm{g}^{k}\big)\right|^{i_{k}}i_{i}!\dots i_{k}!}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}\penalty\ .

The statement of the proposition now follows from the associativity of
\circledcirc
:

\displaystyle((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+z)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}m_{n}\left(\big(((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z)\big)^{\otimes n}\right)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}m_{n}\left(\big((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc((\mathbb{1}+y)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z))\big)^{\otimes n}\right)=(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}((\mathbb{1}+y)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z))\penalty\ .

As shown in the proof of Theorem
2.6
, the product
(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z)
is given by the sum over all
3
-leveled connected graphs, divided by the order of their automorphism groups.
Applying that result to the manipulation from the equation above, we obtain

((\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y))\circledcirc_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbb{1}+z)=\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in 3\textsf{-}\mathsf{dsncGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,y,z)\penalty\ .

∎

2.2.
Graph exponential map

The following new kind of exponential map relates the above two notions of gauge groups.

Definition 2.13

(Total levelisation)
.

A
total levelisation
of a directed simple graph
\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}
is the data of
|\mathrm{g}|
-ordered levels each of which carrying only one vertex and which respects the adjacency condition. We denote the number of total levelisations of a graph by
\ell_{\mathrm{g}}
.

Remark 2.14
.

In the case of rooted trees, this coefficient is called the
Connes–Moscovici coefficient
, see
[
Kre99
,
Bro00
]
.

Definition 2.15

(Graph exponential map)
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathrm{F},\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}})
, the
graph exponential map
is defined by

\begin{array}[]{lccc}\exp\ :&\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}&\to&\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\\
&x&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{1}+\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}_{\mathbb{S}}}\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{g}}}{|\mathrm{g}|!}\,\mathrm{g}(x)\ .\end{array}

\exp(x)\ =\ \mathbb{1}\ +\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to12.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.50018pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.83282pt}{3.53769pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ +\ \scalebox{1.1}{$\frac{1}{2}$}\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to34.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.50018pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Figure 15.
The first terms of the graph exponential map.

So the first terms of the graph exponential are equal to
\mathbb{1}+x+x\star x+\cdots
. Notice that the other terms
cannot
be obtained by the iteration of the Lie-admissible product and thus require the use of the Lie-graph algebra structure. This key observation is the central point of the present paper.
A source of inspiration for the introduction of the present exponential map is the work

[
Cha02
]
of F. Chapoton.

Lemma 2.16
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra, the graph exponential map is bijective,

\exp\ :\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ .

Proof.

The arguments are classical and similar to the proof that a formal power series with unit constant term is invertible. For the sake of completeness, we adapt it here.

First, we prove that the graph exponential map is injective. Let
x
and
y
in
\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
such that
\exp(x)=\exp(y)
.
We will prove, by induction on
k\geqslant 2
, that
x\equiv y\mod\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, that is their projections onto

\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}/\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
agree; this will imply that
x=y
since
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is complete. The image of the graph exponential map is equal to
\exp(x)=\mathbb{1}+\sum_{k\geqslant 1}x^{(k)}
, where
x^{(k)}\in\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is obtained by the action on
x
of a finite sum of directed simple graphs with
k
vertices.
For instance,
x^{(1)}=x
.
The projection of

\exp(x)-\mathbb{1}=x+\sum_{k\geqslant 2}x^{(k)}=\exp(y)-\mathbb{1}=y+\sum_{k\geqslant 2}y^{(k)}
on
\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}/\mathrm{F}_{2}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
shows that there exists
f^{(2)}\in\mathrm{F}_{2}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
such that
x=y+f^{(2)}
. Suppose now that there exists
f^{(k)}\in\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
such that
x=y+f^{(k)}
. The equation
\exp(x)=\exp(y)
implies then

x+y^{(2)}+\cdots+y^{(k)}\equiv y+y^{(2)}+\cdots+y^{(k)}\mod\mathrm{F}_{k+1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, which concludes the induction.

We prove now that the graph exponential map is surjective. Let
x\in\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. We claim that there exists a convergent series
y=x+f^{(2)}+f^{(3)}+\cdots
, with
f^{(k)}\in\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, for any
k\geqslant 2
, satisfying

\exp(y)\equiv\exp\big(x+f^{(2)}+\cdots+f^{(k-1)}\big)\equiv\mathbb{1}+x\mod\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Such a series can be iteratively found as follows. For
k=2
, we have
\exp(x)\equiv\mathbb{1}+x\mod\mathrm{F}_{2}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Let us now suppose that there exists
y=x+f^{(2)}+\cdots+f^{(k-1)}
, with
f^{(i)}\in\mathrm{F}_{i}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, such that

\exp(y)\equiv\exp\big(x+f^{(2)}+\cdots+f^{(j-1)}\big)\equiv\mathbb{1}+x\mod\mathrm{F}_{j}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, for any
2\leqslant j\leqslant k
.
For any
f^{(k)}\in\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, we have

\exp\big(x+f^{(2)}+\cdots+f^{(k)}\big)\equiv\mathbb{1}+x+f^{(k)}+z^{(k)}\mod\mathrm{F}_{k+1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, where
z^{(k)}\in\mathrm{F}_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is given by
the action of a finite sum of directed simple graphs with at most
k
vertices on
x,f^{(2)},\ldots,f^{(k-1)}
. It is thus enough to set
f^{(k)}\coloneq-z^{(k)}
for the equation to hold modulo
\mathrm{F}_{k+1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
.

∎

Definition 2.17

(Graph logarithm map)
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra,
the inverse of the graph exponential map is called the
graph logarithm map
:

\mathrm{log}\ :\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ .

Figure 16.
The first terms of the graph logarithm map.

Remark 2.18
.

The present graph exponential map generalises the pre-Lie exponential map and
the present graph logarithm map generalises the Magnus expansion map, see
[
AG80
,
Man11
]
for instance.
Like in the pre-Lie case
[
EFM09
]
, it would be interesting to unravel a closed formula for the graph logarithm map.

In Lie theory, the exponential map is a key notion, which recovers the local group structure from the infinitesimal Lie structure on on the tangent space; it is characterized by a certain differential equation. The present graph exponential map plays the same role for Lie-graph algebras; we describe its characterizing differential equation below. This Lie theoretical property satisfied by the exponential graph map will play a major role in the proof of the forthcoming
Theorem

2.22
.

Definition 2.19

(
\rhd
-product and
\lhd
-product)
.

Given a complete Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and two elements
x,y\in\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, the elements
(\mathbb{1}+x)\rhd y
and
x\lhd(\mathbb{1}+y)
of
\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
are defined respectively by

(\mathbb{1}+x)\rhd y\coloneq\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to12.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.76753pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Remark 2.20
.

The product
(\mathbb{1}+x)\rhd y
is made up of the summands of
(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)
with only one vertex labelled by
y
, that is its linear part in
y
.
The product
x\lhd(\mathbb{1}+y)
is made up of the summands of
(\mathbb{1}+x)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+y)
with only one vertex labelled by
x
, that is its linear part in
x
.
This explains the choice of notation, which also coincides with the convention of
[
HLV21
, Section 3.3]
.

Lemma 2.21
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, any
z\in\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and any
t\in\mathbb{k}
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\exp(tz)=\exp(tz)\rhd z=z\lhd\exp(tz)\penalty\ .

Proof.

We will only show the first equality, as the second one follows by symmetry. Given a graph
\mathrm{g}
with
|\mathrm{g}|
vertices, we have
\mathrm{g}(tz)=t^{|\mathrm{g}|}\mathrm{g}(z)
. It follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\exp(tz)=\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}}\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{g}}}{(|\mathrm{g}|-1)!}t^{|\mathrm{g}|-1}\,\mathrm{g}(z)\ .

We will give a combinatorial proof that

(
\diamond
)

\exp(tz)\rhd z=\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}}\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{g}}}{(|\mathrm{g}|-1)!}t^{|\mathrm{g}|-1}\,\mathrm{g}(z)\ .

Indeed, the left-hand side is equal to

\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}}\allowbreak\rho_{\mathrm{g}}t^{|\mathrm{g}|-1}\,\mathrm{g}(z)
,
for some coefficients
\rho_{\mathrm{g}}
that we will now compute.

Let
\mathrm{g}\in{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}
be a directed simple graph
and consider one of its total levelisation, say
\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{lev}}
, and let us see how it can appear on the left-hand side of
Equation

⋄

, using arguments similar to the proof of
Theorem

2.6
.
The totally leveled graph
\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{lev}}
admits a top vertex; cutting out its bottom edges attached to it produces
r
totally leveled graphs
\mathrm{g}_{1},\ldots\mathrm{g}_{r}
, with multiplicity, after forgetting the trivial levels without any vertex. These graphs are actually more determined when one considers at which vertices the edges coming from the top vertex is attached to. As above, we call
marked
such totally leveled graphs. In the present case, let us say that the leveled graph
\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{lev}}
induces
l
different marked leveled graphs
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
, for
1\leqslant m\leqslant l
.
Let us denote by
j_{m}
the number of occurrences of the
marked leved graph
\dot{\gamma}_{m}
among
\mathrm{g}_{1},\ldots\mathrm{g}_{r}
, so that
j_{1}+\dots+j_{l}=r
.
One can regroup them as follows. Suppose that there are
k
types of totally leveled graphs among
\mathrm{g}_{1},\ldots\mathrm{g}_{r}
; for any
1\leqslant h\leqslant k
, we denote by
t_{h}
the number of marked leveled graphs appearing among the
\mathrm{g}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
when one forget about the extra edges, so that
t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k}=r
is a coarser partition of
r
.

In the other way around, given such a data, let us see how many times one can get
\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{lev}}
from contributions on the left-hand side. There are 5 different coefficients to take into account.

i)

The overall coefficient
\frac{1}{r!}
.

ii)

The coefficient of the totally leveled graphs
\mathrm{g}_{i}
which is equal to

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{g}_{i}|!}
.

iii)

A multinomial coefficient corresponding to the places where the leveled graphs
\mathrm{g}_{1},\ldots\mathrm{g}_{r}
can be put:

\frac{r!}{t_{1}!\ldots t_{k}!}
.

iv)

A multinomial coefficient corresponding to the places where the marked leveled graphs can be put among the leveled graphs
\mathrm{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{g}_{r}
.
For each
1\leqslant h\leqslant k
, we denote by
j_{m^{h}_{1}},\ldots,j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}
the respective numbers of marked leveled graphs that give the same leveled graph after forgetting the extra incoming edge, so that

j_{m^{h}_{1}}+\cdots+j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}=t_{h}
and
\left\{m^{1}_{1},\ldots,m^{k}_{n(k)}\right\}=\{1,\ldots,l\}
. This produces the coefficient

\prod_{h=1}^{k}\frac{t_{h}!}{j_{m^{h}_{1}}!\ldots j_{m^{h}_{n(h)}}!}=\frac{t_{1}!\dots t_{k}!}{j_{1}!\dots j_{l}!}\penalty\ .

v)

A shuffling coefficient corresponding to the various ways of obtaining the total levelization on
\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{lev}}
from the ones of
\dot{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\dot{\gamma}_{l}
:

\frac{|\mathrm{g}_{1}|!\ldots|\mathrm{g}_{r}|!}{(|\mathrm{g}|-1)!}j_{1}!\ldots,j_{l}!\penalty\ .

Multiplying all the contributions, we get
\frac{1}{(|\mathrm{g}|-1)!}
and thus

\rho_{\mathrm{g}}=\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{g}}}{(|\mathrm{g}|-1)!}\penalty\ .

∎

Theorem 2.22
.

For any complete Lie-graph algebra, the graph exponential and logarithm maps are filtered isomorphisms, thus homeomorphisms,
between the gauge group and the deformation gauge group:

\exp\ :\Gamma=\big(\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\mathrm{BCH},0\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\leftrightarrows}}\left(\mathbb{1}+\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}_{0},\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\right)=\mathfrak{G}\ :\ \mathrm{log}\ .

Proof.

The graph exponential map and the graph logarithm map preserve the respective filtrations by construction.
Lemma

2.16
showed that they are bijective. It remains to show that they preserve the respective group structures; to this extent, we will show that

(
\circ
)

\exp(\mathrm{BCH}(x,y))=\exp(x)\circledcirc\exp(y)\ .

Since we are working over a ground field of characteristic
0
, it is enough to prove this formula for the free complete Lie-graph algebra
\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)
on two elements
x,y
over the field
\mathbb{Q}
of rational numbers.
This free complete Lie-graph algebra admits for underlying space the product

\prod_{\mathrm{g}\in{\mathsf{dsGra}}}\mathbb{Q}\mathrm{g}(x,y)
, where
\mathfrak{g}(x,y)
stands for all the possible ways to label the vertices of the simple graph
\mathfrak{g}
with
x
and
y
.
The filtration
\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)
is made of up products of directed simple graphs with at least
k
vertices labelled by
x
and
y
. Since this Lie-graph algebra is complete, it is enough to prove
Equation

∘

modulo
\mathrm{F}_{k}
, for any
k\geqslant 1
. Let us fix
k
and work with the
\mathbb{R}
-extension of the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space

\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)/\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)
. This way, the data

\left(\mathbb{1}+\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)/\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y),\circledcirc,\mathbb{1}\right)

becomes a finite dimensional Lie group so we can apply to it the classical methods of Lie groups as follows.

Notice now that its tangent space at the unit is
\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)/\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(x,y)
. It is straightforward to see that the Lie bracket induced by
\circledcirc
is equal to the skew-symmetrisation of the Lie-admissible product
\star
given by the action of the 2-vertices graph.
In this real truncated version of the deformation gauge group, the differential of the left multiplication

\Lambda_{\mathbb{1}+v}:\mathbb{1}+w\mapsto(\mathbb{1}+v)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+w)
is equal to

\mathrm{D}_{\mathbb{1}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{1}+v}(z)=\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to12.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0 L 15.75 15.75 L 0 15.75 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

which is the part of
(\mathbb{1}+v)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+z)
made up of only one
z
labelling the top level of the underlying 2-levels graphs.

The (classical) exponential map of this Lie group is the map integrating the flow induced by the vector field
\mathbb{1}+v\mapsto(\mathbb{1}+v)\rhd z
and starting at the unit
\mathbb{1}
.

Lemma

2.21
shows that the graph exponential map
\exp(tz)
satisfies this differential equation, that is

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi(t)=\varphi(t)\rhd z
, with initial condition
\varphi(0)=\mathbb{1}
.
We conclude with the fact that the exponential map of a Lie group is (at least locally) a group morphism from the Hausdorff group defined by the BCH-formula to the Lie group.
∎

Remark 2.23
.

This proof shows that the graph exponential map is
the
expected exponential map from Lie theory.

2.3.
Action on Maurer–Cartan elements

In any dg Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mathrm{d},[\,,])
, one can use the
differential trick

[
DSV24
, Section 1.2]
to reduce
involved computations to similar ones but without the differential map appearing.
More precisely, this amounts to working in the one-dimensional abelian extension Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}^{+}\coloneq(\mathbb{k}\delta\oplus\mathfrak{g},[\,,])

defined by
[\delta,x]\coloneq\mathrm{d}x
and by
[\delta,\delta]\coloneq 0
.
Since the differential of
\mathfrak{g}
has been made internal in
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
, the various formulas of
\mathfrak{g}
have a simpler form in
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
.
For instance, Maurer–Cartan elements of
\mathfrak{g}
are viewed as follows in
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
:

\bar{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}\mapsto\alpha\coloneq\delta+\bar{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}^{+}\penalty\ .

In this way, the Maurer–Cartan equation in
\mathfrak{g}

is equivalent to the square-zero equation in
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
:

[\alpha,\alpha]=0\ .

In the extended complete Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
, the gauge group
\Gamma
acts on the set of Maurer–Cartan elements
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
via the shorter formula

\lambda.\alpha=e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\alpha)\ .

Going back to the original complete dg Lie
\mathfrak{g}
, one recovers this way the classical but more intricate formula for the gauge group action:

\lambda.\bar{\alpha}\coloneq e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\bar{\alpha})+\frac{\mathrm{id}-e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\penalty\ .

In order to perform the same kind of differential trick in complete dg Lie-graph algebras, we cannot simply consider a one-dimensional abelian extension; the relevant extension is conceptually defined as follows.

We consider the category of
internal dg Lie-graph algebras
, which is made up of dg Lie-graph algebras
(A,\mathrm{d},\{\mathrm{g}\}_{\mathrm{g}\in\mathsf{dsGra}})
equipped with a degree
-1
element
\delta
satisfying, for any
x\in A
:

\mathrm{d}x=\mathrm{ad}_{\delta}(x)=\delta\star x-(-1)^{|x|}x\star\delta\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{d}\delta=0\penalty\ ,

where
\star
denote the binary operation in
\mathfrak{g}
coming from the directed simple graph with two vertices.
Notice that the first relation applied to
x=\delta
gives
\mathrm{d}\delta=2\delta\star\delta
, so the second relation is equivalent to
\delta\star\delta=0
.
The morphisms of internal dg Lie-graph algebras are the one of dg Lie-graph algebras preserving the respective extra degree
-1
elements. Over the category of graded modules, the category of dg Lie-graph algebras is encoded by the operad
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
with the differential included in it:

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\coloneq\frac{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\vee\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{d})}{\left(\mathrm{d}\ \text{derivation},\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\right)}\penalty\ ,

where
\mathrm{d}
is a derivation for any operation in
\mathrm{g}\in\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)
, that is

\mathrm{d}\circ_{1}\mathrm{g}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{g}\circ_{i}\mathrm{d}
.
Similarly, the category of internal dg Lie-graph algebras is encoded by the following operad
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
in graded modules which is defined with an extra degree
-1
and arity
0
element:

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\coloneq\frac{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\vee\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{d},\delta)}{\left(\mathrm{d}\ \text{derivation},\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\penalty\ ,\,\mathrm{d}-\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to35.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-1.11476pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The morphism of graded operads
\rho\colon\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\to\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
, preserving
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
and
\mathrm{d}
, induces a pair of adjoint functors by
[
LV12
, Section 5.2.12]
: the right adjoint functor
\rho^{*}
forgets the choice of internal element and the left adjoint
\rho_{!}
produces the differential extension for dg Lie-graph algebras.

Definition 2.24

(Differential extension)
.

The
differential extension
of a complete dg Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is the complete internal dg Lie-graph algebra

\mathfrak{g}^{+}\coloneq\rho_{!}(\mathfrak{g})
.

By a slight abuse of notation, we will simply denote by
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
, and not
\rho^{*}(\mathfrak{g}^{+})
, the complete dg Lie-graph algebra structure on the differential extension.

Proposition 2.25
.

The unit of the
(\rho_{!},\rho^{*})
-adjunction is an embedding of complete dg Lie-graph algebras:

\mathfrak{g}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{g}^{+}
.

Proof.

We first claim that the morphism
\rho\colon\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
of graded operads is injective.
We consider the increasing filtration on the operad
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
given by the number of elements
\delta
involved.
The associated graded operad admits the following presentation

\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\cong\frac{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\vee\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{d},\delta)}{\left(\mathrm{d}\ \text{derivation},\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\penalty\ ,\,\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to35.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-1.11476pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.46825pt}{24.9801pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ ,\mathrm{d}(\delta)\right)}\penalty\ .

This shows that its part of weight
0
in
\delta
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{gr}_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\cong\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
.
Since the underlying two
\mathbb{S}
-modules of the operads
\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
and
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
are isomorphic, this proves the claim.

We recall then that the functor
\rho_{!}
is given by the relative composite product

\rho_{!}(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}_{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}\,\mathfrak{g}\penalty\ .

Since we are working over a field
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic
0
, every symmetry group algebra
\mathbb{k}[\mathbb{S}_{n}]
is semisimple, so the underlying graded
\mathbb{S}
-module
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\oplus\mathrm{M}
. This implies that

\mathfrak{g}^{+}\cong\mathfrak{g}\oplus V
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

The above arguments apply as well to the Lie and pre-Lie cases.
The Lie case is more simple than the Lie-graph case, since we can make this relative composite product explicit in this context:

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}_{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}}\,\mathfrak{g}\cong\frac{\mathfrak{g}\vee\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}}(\delta)}{\big([\delta,\delta],[\delta,x]=\mathrm{d}x\big)}\cong\mathbb{k}\delta\oplus\mathfrak{g}\ ,

which recovers the one-dimensional abelian extension mentioned above. (One can prove directly that this new complete internal dg Lie algebra satisfies the universal property of the left adjoint functor.)

Remark 2.26
.

In contrast to the Lie case, it is difficult to give an expression for

\mathfrak{g}^{+}
more explicit than
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}_{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}\,\mathfrak{g}
. In what comes next, we do not actually need any explicit form nor basis for the differential extension, only the fact that the original dg Lie-graph algebra embeds in it.

The following construction will allow us to make explicit the action of the deformation gauge group on Maurer–Cartan elements.

Definition 2.27

(Bowtie graph)
.

A
bowtie graph
is a 3-leveled directed simple graphs with only one vertex on the middle level and possibly empty top and bottom levels. We denote the set of bowtie graphs by
{\Join}\textsf{-}{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}
.

Definition 2.28

(Bowtie element)
.

For any elements
x,y\in\mathrm{F}_{1}\mathfrak{g}
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}
in a complete Lie-graph algebra, we consider the associated
bowtie element

of
\mathfrak{g}
:

(\mathbb{1}+x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}(\mathbb{1}+y)\coloneq\sum_{\mathrm{g}\in{\Join}\textsf{-}{\mathsf{dsGra}}_{\mathbb{S}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{g})|}}\,\mathrm{g}(x,\alpha,y)\ ,

where the notation
\mathrm{g}(x,\alpha,y)
stands for the action in
\mathfrak{g}
of the bowtie graph
\mathrm{g}
with

x
labelling the bottom vertices,
\alpha
the middle vertex, and
y
the top vertices, see
Figure

17
.

\displaystyle(\mathbb{1}+x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}(\mathbb{1}+y)\ =\

Figure 17.
The first terms of the bowtie element

(\mathbb{1}+x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\Join}}(\mathbb{1}+y)
.

Theorem 2.29
.

For any complete dg Lie-graph algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the deformation gauge group
\mathfrak{G}
acts on the set
\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g})
of Maurer–Cartan elements via the formula

\exp(\lambda)\cdot\alpha\coloneq\lambda.\alpha=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)\ ,

in the differential extension
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
, and via the formula

\displaystyle\exp(\lambda)\cdot\bar{\alpha}\coloneq\lambda.\bar{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\alpha}}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)-\left(\exp(\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(\lambda)\big)\right)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)

\displaystyle=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\alpha}}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)+\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc\left(\exp(-\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(-\lambda)\big)\right)\ ,

in
\mathfrak{g}
, where the notation
\left(x;y\right)
means that we label all the vertices of the level by
x
except for one which is labelled by
y
.

Proof.

The deformation gauge group action comes from the gauge group action via the exponential map, which is a morphism by
Theorem

2.22
, so it defines a genuine group action.

The first formula amounts to prove

e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\alpha)=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)\penalty\ .

To this extent, we use the same method as in the proof of
Theorem

2.22
:
it is enough to prove this for the free complete Lie-graph algebra

\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha,\lambda)

over
\mathbb{Q}
on two generators of respective degrees
|\alpha|=-1
and
|\lambda|=0
.
Since this latter one is complete, it is enough to prove it for its truncated version

\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha,\lambda)/\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha,\lambda)
,
for any
k\geqslant 1
. For any fixed
k
, we work with its real extension

\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha,\lambda)/\mathrm{F}_{k}\widehat{\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha,\lambda)
, which is a finite-dimensional real graded Lie algebra.

For any finite dimensional real graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
, the element
e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\alpha)
is the unique solution at
t=1
of the differential equation

\frac{d}{dt}\psi(t)=\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}(\psi(t))
, with initial condition
\psi(0)=\alpha
, in the algebraic variety

\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{h})
. To conclude, it is enough to show that

\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)

satisfies this differential equation, that is

\frac{d}{dt}\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)=\lambda\star\left(\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\right)-\left(\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\right)\star\lambda\penalty\ .

First, the bowtie element at
t=0
is equal to
\exp(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(0)=\alpha
.
Then, we recall, from the proof of

Theorem

2.22
, that

\frac{d}{dt}\exp(t\lambda)=\exp(t\lambda)\rhd\lambda=\lambda\lhd\exp(t\lambda)\penalty\ ,

where the latter notion
\lhd
is vertical mirror to the former notion
\rhd
.
This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)=

\displaystyle\big(\exp(t\lambda);\lambda\lhd\exp(t\lambda)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)-

\displaystyle\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\big(\exp(-t\lambda);\exp(-t\lambda)\rhd\lambda\big)\ .

Finally we claim that

(2.30)

\displaystyle\big(\exp(t\lambda);\lambda\lhd\exp(t\lambda)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)=\lambda\star\left(\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\right)\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\big(\exp(-t\lambda);\exp(-t\lambda)\rhd\lambda\big)=\left(\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\right)\star\lambda\penalty\ ,

which would conclude the proof.

Let us now show the first equation of (
2.30
); the proof of the other one being similar. The associativity relation (
Theorem

2.6
) of
the product
\circledcirc
gives

\big((\mathbb{1}+\lambda)\circledcirc\exp(t\lambda)\big)\circledcirc\big((\mathbb{1}+\alpha)\circledcirc\exp(-t\lambda)\big)=(\mathbb{1}+\lambda)\circledcirc\big(\exp(t\lambda)\big)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}+\alpha)\circledcirc\exp(-t\lambda)\big)\penalty\ ,

which is given by the sum over the set of 4-leveled directed simple graphs.
The projection of this equation onto the sum over the directed simple graphs with only one vertex on the first and on the third level gives

\displaystyle\big(\exp(t\lambda);\lambda\lhd\exp(t\lambda)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda\lhd\big(\exp(t\lambda)\big)\circledcirc\exp(-t\lambda),\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\star\left(\exp(t\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-t\lambda)\right)\penalty\ ,

since
\exp(t\lambda)\big)\circledcirc\exp(-t\lambda)=\mathbb{1}
.

In order to settle the second formula, it remains to show that

\delta-e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\delta)=\delta-\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)=\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}-\mathrm{id}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\lambda}}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)=\left(\exp(\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(\lambda)\big)\right)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)\penalty\ ,

where the first equalities are already known to hold in
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
. To this extent, we use the same kind of idea as above: we consider the projection of the associativity relation (
Theorem

2.6
)

(\mathbb{1}+\delta)\circledcirc\big(\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}-\delta)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)\big)=\big((\mathbb{1}+\delta)\circledcirc\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc(\mathbb{1}-\delta)\big)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)\penalty\ ,

onto the summand made up of only one
\delta
. The left-hand side gives

\delta-\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)

and the right-hand side gives

\left(\exp(\lambda);\delta\lhd\exp{\lambda}-\exp(\lambda)\rhd\delta\right)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)\penalty\ .

We claim that
\delta\lhd\exp{\lambda}-\exp(\lambda)\rhd=\delta\star\exp{\lambda}-\exp(\lambda)\star\delta=\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(\lambda)\big)
, which would conclude the proof.

This relation comes from the fact that

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to77.1pt{\vbox to45.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.96472pt\lower-30.27646pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.33 10.33}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

The derivation relation endows the operad
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
with a distributive law
\mathrm{D}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\to\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}
, where
\mathrm{D}\coloneq\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{d})/\big(\mathrm{d}^{2}\big)
stands for the algebra of dual numbers. This proves that its underlying graded
\mathbb{S}
-module is isomorphic to
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\cong\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}
. The same property holds for the operad
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
, whose underlying graded
\mathbb{S}
-module is isomorphic to

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}\cong\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}\cong\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}\oplus\overline{\mathrm{Com}}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}\penalty\ ,

where
\overline{\mathrm{Com}}\cong\mathrm{Com}/\mathrm{I}
. This shows that the canonical morphism of operads

\bar{\eta}\colon\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\hookrightarrow\allowbreak\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}
is injective. It remains to show that its extension
\eta
with a internal degree
-1

0
element is still injective. To this extent, we consider the increasing filtration on
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
defined by the number of connected components or equivalently by the arity of the sub-operad
\mathrm{Com}
.
We claim that the underlying
\mathbb{S}
-module of the associated graded operad is isomorphic to

\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}\cong\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\cong\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\oplus\overline{\mathrm{Com}}\circ\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}\penalty\ ,

which would conclude the injectivity of the morphism
\eta
since the underlying
\mathbb{S}
-modules satisfy
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}\cong\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
.

inside
\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}\circ\mathrm{D}\vee\mathcal{T}(\delta)
is made up of two types of terms.
The first ones are given by non-necessarily connected graphs with at least one relation above inserted at one vertex. This preserves the connected components.
The second ones are given by non-necessarily connected graphs inserted at the only vertex of the first displayed relation above.

\mathrm{d}

fails to be a derivation in
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
because it might create terms where several connected components are linked to a
\delta
, but such terms are killed in
\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
by the definition of the filtration.
So this gives rise to a derivation in

\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
which distributes too over the connected components of the graph.
In the end, this shows that the above ideal preserves the connected components of the graphs in the operad
\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}
, which implies the isomorphism

\mathrm{gr}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{ncgra}^{+}\cong\mathrm{Com}\circ\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}^{+}
.

Using the same arguments as above, namely
Proposition

2.25
, this gives rise to a pair of adjoint functors
\eta_{!}
and
\eta^{*}
whose unit

\mathfrak{g}^{+}\hookrightarrow\eta^{*}\big(\eta_{!}\big(\mathfrak{g}^{+}\big)\big)
is an embedding of complete internal dg Lie-graph algebras.

n\geqslant 2
in the latter one. For

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 48.23 41.34 L 10.33 20.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\penalty\ .

Finally, the symmetric formula

\displaystyle\exp(\lambda)\cdot\bar{\alpha}=\exp(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\alpha}}}{{\vbox{\hbox{\text{\scalebox{1.2}{$\Join$}}}}}}\exp(-\lambda)+\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc\left(\exp(-\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(-\lambda)\big)\right)

can either been proved by the same arguments applied from the beginning in a symmetrical way or as follows.
We apply the differential
\mathrm{d}
to the element

\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)=\mathbb{1}\penalty\ ,

which gives

\left(\exp(\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(\lambda)\big)\right)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)+\exp(\lambda)\circledcirc\left(\exp(-\lambda);\mathrm{d}\big(\exp(-\lambda)\big)\right)=0\penalty\ .

∎

Remark 2.31
.

If we restrict the first formula of
Theorem

2.29
to rooted trees, we recover precisely the formula
(\exp(\lambda)\star\alpha)\circledcirc\exp(-\lambda)
obtained in
[
DSV16
]
for pre-Lie algebras.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
1.13

Proposition

1.13
states that the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is not finitely generated.
Recall that a presentation by generators and relations of an operad
\mathcal{P}
is an isomorphism of the form
\mathcal{P}\cong\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)
. The operad
\mathcal{P}
is said to be
finitely generated
if the generating space
E
can be chosen to be of total finite dimension.
Since we are dealing with the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
that is trivial in arities
0
and
1
and is arity-wise finite dimensional, the only potential source of infinite dimensionality phenomena is that
E(n)
stays non-trivial as
n
goes to infinity. Concretely, we will use the following two general propositions.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad such that
\dim\mathcal{P}(n)<+\infty
, for all
n
. Suppose that
\mathcal{P}
can be generated by an

\mathbb{S}
-module
E
for which there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
E(n)=0
, for
n>N
. Then, the operad
\mathcal{P}
is finitely generated.

Proof.

We can take as a new
\mathbb{S}
-module of generators
F
defined by
F(n)\coloneq\mathcal{P}(n)
, for
n\leqslant N
, and
F(n)\coloneq 0
otherwise.
Using the isomorphism
\mathcal{P}\cong\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)
, we consider the canonical map of
\mathbb{S}
-modules
g\colon F\to\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)
which is an isomorphism in arities
n\leqslant N
. The morphism of
\mathbb{S}
-modules defined by

f(n)\ \colon E(n)\to\mathcal{T}(E)(n)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)(n)\xrightarrow{g(n)^{-1}}F(n)

for
n\leqslant N
, and
f(n)\coloneq 0
otherwise, induces a morphism of operads

\mathcal{T}(f)\ \colon\mathcal{T}(E)\to\mathcal{T}(F)
which satisfies the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\penalty\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

This shows that the morphism of operads
\mathcal{T}(F)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is surjective.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{T}(E)/(R)
be a finitely generated presentation of an operad
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathcal{P}(0)=0
and
\mathcal{P}(1)=\mathbb{k}I
. Then, there exists a finitely generated presentation
\mathcal{P}\cong\mathcal{T}(F)/(S)
with
F(0)=F(1)=0
.

Proof.

The arguments are the same as above: we consider the generating
\mathbb{S}
-module
F
defined
to be the truncation of
E
to arities
n\geqslant 2
.
One readily checks that the composite

\mathcal{T}(E^{\prime})\to\mathcal{T}(E)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{T}(E)}/{(R)}

is surjective.
∎

Thanks to the previous propositions, it suffices to show the following variant of Proposition
1.13
.

Proposition A.3
.

Suppose that there exists a surjective morphism of operads
\mathcal{T}(E)\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
, with
E
an
\mathbb{S}
-module
concentrated in arities
\geqslant 2
. Then, there exist arbitrarily large
n
such that
E(n)\neq 0
.

Proof of
Proposition

1.13
.

Recall the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
satisfies
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(0)=0
,
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(1)=\mathbb{k}I
. Furthermore
\dim\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)\leq|\text{oriented simple graphs on }n\text{ vertices}|=3^{\binom{n}{2}}<+\infty
, for all
n\geqslant 1
.
Suppose that the operad
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}
is finitely generated. By
Proposition

A.2
, we can suppose that the
generating
\mathbb{S}
-module
E
of total finite dimension
is trivial in arities
0
and
1
. Then
Proposition

A.3
ensures that
there exist arbitrarily large
n
such that
E(n)\neq 0
, which violates the initial finiteness assumption.
∎

We start by estimating a lower bound for the dimension of
\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)
, which is to say, the cardinality of
\mathsf{dsGra}(n)
.

Lemma A.4
.

There exists a constant
c>1
such that
|\mathsf{dsGra}(n)|\geqslant c^{n^{2}}
, for all sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

Consider the linear graph

\hbox to17.87pt{\vbox to103.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-17.47182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.0359pt}{73.60101pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\in\mathsf{dsGra}(n)

and notice that we can add any single edge from the vertex
i
to
j
, for
i\leqslant j-2
, and produce a valid graph in
\mathsf{dsGra}(n)
. Since there are
\binom{n}{2}-n+1
such possible edges, we have

|\mathsf{dsGra}(n)|>2^{\binom{n}{2}-n+1}\penalty\ ,

which is eventually bigger than
c^{n^{2}}
, for
c<\sqrt{2}
. ∎

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

The strategy of the proof is a dimension argument. Assume by contradiction that there exists a generating space
E
and
N>0
such that
E(n)=0
, for
n>N
.
By
Proposition

A.1
, we can further assume that
E(n)=\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)
, for any
n\leqslant N
.
We will show that for
n
large enough
\dim\mathcal{T}(E)(n)
will be smaller than
\dim\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)
, thus establishing the result.

Since
E(0)=0
, a basis of the free operad on
E
is parameterized by shuffle trees: concretely,

\mathcal{T}(E)(n)\cong\bigoplus_{t\in\mathsf{ShTr}(n)}E^{\otimes|t|},

where
\mathsf{ShTr}(n)
denotes the set of shuffle trees with
n
leaves and where
|t|
is the number of the vertices of
t
.
Let
b\coloneq\max\{\dim\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(n)\ |\ n\leqslant N\}
and such maximum is attained in arity
k
. Denoting

{\mathsf{ShTr}^{\leqslant N}(n)}
the set of shuffle trees in which all vertices have arity between
2
and
N
, we deduce

(A.5)

\dim\mathcal{T}(E)(n)\leqslant\dim\bigoplus_{\mathsf{ShTr}^{\leqslant N}(n)}\mathrm{Lie}\textrm{-}\mathrm{gra}(k)^{\otimes n-1}\leqslant\left|\mathsf{ShTr}^{\leqslant N}(n)\right|\times b^{n-1}

from
E(n)=0
, for
n>N
.

To show that this value is eventually smaller than the one from Lemma
A.4
, we need to find an upper bound for the cardinal of
{\mathsf{ShTr}^{\leqslant N}(n)}
. Recall that a shuffle tree is a rooted planar tree together with a permutation of
\{1,\dots,n\}
satisfying some condition.
The number of rooted planar trees with
n
leaves
and with vertices of arity greater or equal to 2
is given by the Schröder–Hipparchus numbers
s_{n}
, which satisfy the following recurrence formula

s_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\left((6n-9)s_{n-1}-(n-3)s_{n-2}\right)\penalty\ ,

see
[
Sta97
]
. By induction, we get that
s_{n}\leqslant 6^{n}
, for all
n
.
The permutations indexing a shuffle tree are a certain subset of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
, so we the following upper bound

\dim\mathcal{T}(E)(n)\leqslant 6^{n}\times n!\times b^{n-1}\penalty\ .

Stirling’s formula provides us with the following asymptotical estimation
n!\sim\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}
.
It follows that

(A.6)

\dim\mathcal{T}(E)(n)\leqslant 6^{n}\times\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}\times b^{n}\leqslant(an)^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\penalty\ ,

for large enough n and for some constant
a>1
.
Taking logarithms, we see that the estimate
c^{n^{2}}
from Lemma
A.4
eventually overtakes
(an)^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
which gives us the desired contradiction.
∎

Remark A.7
.

The proof above by dimension counting boils down to the fact that while trees grow at most like
n^{n}
, the present operad of graphs grows at least like
c^{n^{2}}
. The same kind of argument shows that other types of graph operads appearing in the literature cannot be finitely generated.
